_tool_result:_
```
1	# 307 — C language spec implementation
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	C boxes today run via `drivers/c.sh`, which compiles the source on
8	demand (mtime-based recompile to a binary), then spawns the binary as
9	a subprocess and reads stdout. Process spawn cost dominates; each call
10	is its own freshly-launched executable. The phase 2 driver compiles
11	to a standalone binary, not a `.so`.
12	
13	## Concept
14	The C language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for C. It is
15	the second reference implementation, and the first one that exercises
16	the `compile` callback. After compile, invocation is in-process via
17	`dlopen` / `dlsym` — the user's compiled function is called directly
18	through a function pointer, with no subprocess and no IPC.
19	
20	This is the "Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI" model from
21	`docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`, applied to C: same-process call,
22	nanosecond-overhead invocation, native-typed access. The only thing
23	the C spec adds over the Lua spec is the compile step.
24	
25	## Per-worker state
26	
27	`init` returns a small struct holding the dlopen-handle cache:
28	
29	```c
30	typedef struct {
31	    void  *so_handles[MAX_BOXES];   // per-file .so handle, indexed by file id
32	    void  *fn_ptrs   [MAX_BOXES];   // per-(file, fn) cached function pointer
33	    int    n_cached;
34	    // (a hash table could replace these arrays if MAX_BOXES grows large)
35	} c_handle_t;
36	
37	void *c_init(int worker_idx);
38	void  c_teardown(void *handle);    // dlclose every cached handle
39	```
40	
41	Each worker thread has its own cache. The same `.so` will end up
42	loaded once per worker, since `dlopen` with the same path on the same
43	process returns a refcounted handle (the underlying code segment is
44	shared across all callers — the per-worker `dlopen` is just a
45	refcount bump after the first).
46	
47	## Compile callback
48	
49	The C spec's `compile` callback receives the box's metadata in
50	addition to the source and output paths, so it knows the function's
51	signature and can generate the wrapper:
52	
53	```c
54	int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path, const box_t *box);
55	```
56	
57	(This is a small extension to `lang_spec_t` in issue 303 — `compile`
58	gains a `box_t` pointer. Lua's compile is NULL, so unaffected.)
59	
60	The callback:
61	1. Reads `box->inputs`, `box->returns`, `box->headers`, `box->link_libs`,
62	   `box->cflags` from the box metadata.
63	2. Generates a wrapper `.c` file (see "Wrapper generation" below) in
64	   the map's `compiled/bin/` directory next to where the `.so` will
65	   land.
66	3. Forks and execs the system `cc` with:
67	   - `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall` (defaults)
68	   - The user's `cflags`, if any
69	   - The wrapper `.c` and the user's `src_path` (if `ref` is set)
70	   - `-o out_path`
71	   - `-l<lib>` for each entry in `box->link_libs`
72	4. Waits for the compiler, returns 0 on success, 1 on failure.
73	
74	If the resulting `.so` is newer than the user's source and the
75	wrapper, compile is skipped (mtime check, same as phase 2).
76	
77	The compiler is `cc` — the system default. `gcc` on most Linux,
78	`clang` on macOS. No SoraMech-specific compiler dependency.
79	
80	### cflags in box JSON
81	The `cflags` field on the box JSON is a string of additional compiler
82	flags (`-O0 -g`, `-mavx2`, etc.). It is set by the user in the
83	inspector — a "Compile flags" text input shown when a C-language box
84	is selected. The editor saves it as part of the box's JSON; reads
85	preserve the field; saves write it back. Nothing overwrites it.
86	
87	## Box file convention: any C function, no boilerplate
88	
89	**The rule, stated as a rule:** a box author writes code as if
90	SoraMech does not exist. The function they write must be a
91	function their non-SoraMech compiler can compile, their
92	non-SoraMech editor can lint, their non-SoraMech test harness
93	can call. No SoraMech-prefixed macros. No SoraMech-shaped
94	return types. No `#include "soramech.h"`. Nothing in the
95	author's source betrays that the function will ever cross
96	SoraMech's wire.
97	
98	SoraMech bends to the language. The language never bends to
99	SoraMech. This is non-negotiable across every spec; it's the
100	reason for the spec interface in the first place.
101	
102	Users do not write a SoraMech-specific C signature. They reference
103	any C function — whether one they wrote themselves, or one from a
104	library (zlib, OpenCV, libcurl, anything they have headers for) —
105	and the C spec generates a thin wrapper that bridges SoraMech's
106	bytes-in / bytes-out ABI to the function's natural typed C
107	signature.
108	
109	### What the user provides
110	Either:
111	- A `.c` file containing the function, referenced by `ref` in the
112	  box JSON; or
113	- A library function from a header, no `.c` file of their own — the
114	  `ref` field is omitted, the function name and headers are
115	  declared in the box JSON, and the library is named in
116	  `link_libs`.
117	
118	### Box JSON for a user-written function
119	
120	```json
121	{
122	  "id": "classify",
123	  "kind": "call",
124	  "lang": "c",
125	  "ref": "classify.c",
126	  "fn": "classify",
127	  "inputs": [
128	    {"name": "text",   "type": "string"},
129	    {"name": "weight", "type": "int"}
130	  ],
131	  "returns": "int"
132	}
133	```
134	
135	The user's `.c` file is naturally written:
136	
137	```c
138	// maps/<name>/src/classify.c
139	int classify(const char *text, int weight) {
140	    if (text[0] >= '0' && text[0] <= '9') return weight * 1;
141	    return weight * 2;
142	}
143	```
144	
145	No header to include, no macro, no `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS)`.
146	Just a function with a typed signature.
147	
148	### Box JSON for a library function (no user source)
149	
150	```json
151	{
152	  "id": "compress",
153	  "kind": "call",
154	  "lang": "c",
155	  "fn": "compress2",
156	  "headers": ["<zlib.h>"],
157	  "link_libs": ["z"],
158	  "inputs": [
159	    {"name": "src",   "type": "bytes"},
160	    {"name": "level", "type": "int"}
161	  ],
162	  "returns": "bytes"
163	}
164	```
165	
166	The wrapper includes `<zlib.h>`, calls `compress2`, and the linker
167	pulls in `libz` via `-lz`. No user-written `.c` file.
168	
169	### Wrapper generation
170	
171	The compile callback emits a wrapper `.c` for each C box. The
172	wrapper has a fixed entry-point name (`sm_box_entry`) that the spec
173	always `dlsym`s. It declares the user's function (extern), unpacks
174	SoraMech's input bytes into typed C arguments per the box's `inputs`
175	declaration, calls the user's function, and writes the return value
176	into `sm_out_buf` per the box's `returns` declaration.
177	
178	For `classify` above, the generated wrapper is roughly:
179	
180	```c
181	// generated: classify_wrap.c
182	extern int classify(const char *, int);
183	
184	int sm_box_entry(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
185	                 void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) {
186	    const char *text   = (const char *)inputs[0];
187	    int         weight = atoi((const char *)inputs[1]);
188	    int         result = classify(text, weight);
189	    *out_size = snprintf(out_buf, out_cap, "%d", result);
190	    return 0;
191	}
192	```
193	
194	The wrapper is mechanical — same template for every box, varying
195	only in the input-marshalling lines and the return-encoding line.
196	The compile step writes it to `compiled/bin/<box-id>_wrap.c` and
197	compiles `<box-id>_wrap.c` plus the user's source (if any) into a
198	single `.so`.
199	
200	This means the C spec implements typed function calls without
201	libffi (which would do the marshalling at runtime via the C ABI
202	metadata). The same effect is achieved at compile time with
203	straightforward generated code, no runtime dependency.
204	
205	## Input marshalling (in the generated wrapper)
206	
207	The wrapper converts SoraMech's `(bytes, size)` input pairs into the
208	typed C arguments the user's function expects, based on the
209	declared input types in the box JSON:
210	
211	| Declared type | Wrapper action                                                  |
212	|---------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
213	| `string`      | `(const char *)inputs[i]`                                       |
214	| `int`         | `atoi((const char *)inputs[i])`                                 |
215	| `long`        | `strtoll(...)`                                                  |
216	| `double`      | `strtod(...)`                                                   |
217	| `bool`        | `strcmp("true", inputs[i]) == 0`                                |
218	| `bytes`       | `(const void *)inputs[i]`, paired with `sizes[i]` for the size  |
219	| `json`        | `cJSON_ParseWithLength(inputs[i], sizes[i])` → `cJSON *`        |
220	
221	The `json` case uses the same vendored cJSON the graph loader uses
222	(issue 305) — one JSON library shared across the runner. When a box
223	input is declared `"type": "json"`, the wrapper passes a `cJSON *`
224	to the user's function. The user navigates the tree with cJSON's
225	API (`cJSON_GetObjectItem`, `cJSON_GetArrayItem`, etc.) and the
226	wrapper frees the parsed tree after the function returns.
227	
228	If a parse fails (a `json` input that is not valid JSON, an `int`
229	input that does not parse as an integer), the wrapper aborts the
230	program with the box id and the input position (per issue 303).
231	
232	## Output encoding (in the generated wrapper)
233	
234	The wrapper takes the user function's return value and serializes
235	it into `sm_out_buf`, based on the declared `returns` type:
236	
237	| Declared `returns` | Wrapper action                                              |
238	|--------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
239	| `string`           | the user returns `const char *`; `strcpy` + set `out_size` |
240	| `int`              | `snprintf("%d", ret)`                                       |
241	| `long`             | `snprintf("%lld", ret)`                                     |
242	| `double`           | `snprintf("%.17g", ret)`                                    |
243	| `bool`             | write `"true"` or `"false"`                                 |
244	| `bytes`            | the user returns `(void *, int)` via an out-parameter pair (see below) |
245	| `json`             | the user returns `cJSON *`; `cJSON_PrintUnformatted` → bytes |
246	| `void`             | `*out_size = 0` (zero-byte output)                          |
247	
248	For `bytes` returns, the user function's signature has the form
249	`int my_fn(... const char *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size)` —
250	extra parameters that the wrapper passes through from
251	`sm_out_buf` / `sm_out_capacity` / `sm_out_size`. This handles
252	library functions like `compress` that write their output via an
253	out-pointer rather than a return value. The box JSON declares
254	`"returns": "bytes"` and the wrapper threads the buffer through.
255	
256	If the encoded value exceeds `sm_out_capacity`, the wrapper aborts
257	(per issue 303). For boxes whose output capacity is not known at
258	compile time, see "Variable-size outputs" below — the wrapper
259	routes through the large-value heap.
260	
261	## Variable-size outputs
262	
263	For boxes whose output capacity is not known at compile time (issue
264	302's large-value heap): the box JSON declares `output_capacity = 0`
265	and the wrapper allocates from the large-value heap on each call.
266	For `bytes` and `json` returns, the wrapper transparently picks the
267	heap path when the box is variable-size — the user's function is
268	unchanged either way. The handle (pointer + size into the
269	large-value heap) is what ends up in `sm_out_buf`.
270	
271	For string returns, similarly: if the returned string exceeds the
272	declared fixed `output_capacity`, the wrapper falls back to the
273	large-value heap. This decision is made at compile time based on the
274	box JSON, not at runtime.
275	
276	## Thread safety of user code
277	
278	The same `.so` is loaded by every worker thread that may call into
279	it. **Static variables and globals declared in the user's C source
280	are shared mutable state across all workers** unless explicitly
281	made `const`.
282	
283	A note on `static` in C: it does not mean immutable. `static` at
284	file scope means "this symbol has internal linkage, only visible
285	inside this `.c` file." `static` inside a function means "this
286	variable persists across calls." Either way, the variable itself is
287	mutable — `static int counter = 0; counter++;` is perfectly legal
288	and does what it says, just with internal linkage. Immutable in C
289	is `const`.
290	
291	So:
292	- `static int counter = 0;` at file scope — racy, shared across workers.
293	- `static int x = 0;` inside a function body — racy, shared across workers calling that function.
294	- `const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.
295	- `static const int max = 100;` — safe, immutable.
296	
297	Box functions must be reentrant — no mutable shared state without
298	explicit locking. The C spec does not enforce this; the rule lives
299	in `docs/005-language-specs.md` (to be written) for box authors to
300	read.
301	
302	### Library functions that need global init
303	
304	Many real C libraries require a one-time setup call before any of
305	their functions work — `curl_global_init` for libcurl,
306	`OPENSSL_init_crypto` for OpenSSL, etc. A SoraMech box that calls
307	into such a library cannot just be wired in cold; the library's
308	state has to exist before the call.
309	
310	Two patterns handle this without changing the spec interface:
311	
312	1. **Init box wired as an entry point.** The user creates a small C
313	   box whose function is the library's init call (e.g.
314	   `curl_global_init`). They mark it as an entry box and wire it
315	   such that any box using libcurl is downstream of it. The init
316	   runs once at startup before any consumer fires. Process-global
317	   library state — which is what most C libraries actually use — is
318	   set up by the time anyone needs it. Pairs with a teardown box
319	   wired as a terminal node if needed.
320	
321	2. **Per-worker init via spec extension** (future). For libraries
322	   that need per-worker setup (each thread gets its own handle),
323	   the C spec gains an optional per-box `worker_init_fn` field in
324	   the box JSON. The spec calls that function once per worker
325	   thread during pool startup, before any box invoke. Not in scope
326	   yet — pattern (1) covers the common case.
327	
328	The init-box approach is the recommended starting point. The
329	spec-extension path is the escape hatch when (1) does not fit.
330	
331	A future fallback exists: `dlmopen`, a Linux variant of `dlopen`
332	that loads each library into its own isolated "namespace," giving
333	each worker its own copy of the library's symbols (statics
334	included). Two threads using two different namespaces would not
335	share static state. Less portable than `dlopen` (Linux-only), so
336	not the default — flagged here as the escape hatch if static-
337	variable races become a real problem.
338	
339	## Build
340	
341	```
342	langs/c/
343	    spec.c              ← lang_spec_t implementation
344	    soramech-c.h        ← user-facing header (shipped to maps via compile step)
345	    Makefile            ← builds spec.so
346	    spec.so             ← built artifact (gitignored)
347	```
348	
349	Spec Makefile target:
350	```make
351	spec.so: spec.c
352		$(CC) -shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall -o $@ $< -ldl
353	```
354	
355	Linking against `libdl` for `dlopen` / `dlsym` is the only
356	dependency. No interpreter, no helper library beyond libc.
357	
358	### What `dlopen` and `dlsym` do
359	- `dlopen("path/to/file.so", flags)` loads a shared library at
360	  runtime. The OS loader maps the library's code into the process's
361	  memory and returns a handle. Same idea as Windows'
362	  `LoadLibrary`.
363	- `dlsym(handle, "name")` looks up a symbol — a function or a
364	  variable — by name inside an already-loaded library, and returns
365	  a pointer to it. Same idea as Windows' `GetProcAddress`.
366	
367	Together they are how a program loads and calls code that wasn't
368	linked at compile time. Plugin systems are the classic use case.
369	Here, every C box compiles to its own `.so`; the spec `dlopen`s the
370	`.so` once per worker, `dlsym`s `sm_box_entry` once per file, and
371	calls the resulting function pointer per invocation.
372	
373	The user's C box files are compiled by the `compile` callback at map
374	load time, not by this Makefile. The Makefile here builds only the
375	spec itself.
376	
377	## Error handling
378	
379	- `dlopen` failure (missing `.so`, link errors): print path + `dlerror()`
380	  to stderr, return 1, dispatch layer aborts.
381	- `dlsym` failure (missing function): print file/fn + `dlerror()`,
382	  return 1, abort.
383	- Compile failure: `cc` exit nonzero, print compiler stderr (already
384	  routed there by `cc`), return 1, abort.
385	- User function returns nonzero: propagate that as the spec's return,
386	  abort.
387	
388	No retry, no fallback. Per issue 303.
389	
390	## Open questions
391	
392	(none currently — earlier questions resolved as follows:)
393	
394	- Per-box `cflags`: yes, add the field to the box JSON. The
395	  inspector shows a "Compile flags" text input when a C-language
396	  box is selected (phase 2 inspector work).
397	- Typed signatures: handled via compile-time wrapper generation
398	  (see "Box file convention" above). No libffi runtime dependency.
399	- Static-variable isolation via `dlmopen`: deferred. Current rule
400	  is "C box authors write reentrant code"; if that breaks down in
401	  practice, fall back to per-worker `dlmopen` namespaces (Linux-only).
402	
403	## Suggested implementation sequence
404	
405	1. Set up `langs/c/` directory with a Makefile that builds an empty
406	   `spec.so` exporting a stub `soramech_lang_spec`.
407	2. Implement `init` and `teardown` with the dlopen-handle cache.
408	3. Implement `compile` — fork/exec `cc` with the standard flags.
409	   Smoke test: compile a single hello-world `.c` file.
410	4. Implement `invoke` — dlsym, direct call, propagate return.
411	5. Write `soramech-c.h` with the `SM_BOX_ARGS` macro and the basic
412	   input/output helpers.
413	6. Smoke test: a C box that takes one string input, writes a string
414	   output, runs end-to-end through the pool.
415	7. Add the typed input helpers (`sm_in_int`, `sm_in_double`, etc.).
416	8. Add the typed output helpers.
417	9. Test against `maps/driver-test` (C portions).
418	10. Add variable-size output support (`sm_out_alloc`, `sm_out_handle`).
419	11. Document in `docs/005-language-specs.md` once it exists, with a
420	    note on thread safety.
421	
422	## Relevant files
423	
424	- `drivers/c.sh` — phase 2 C driver, retired by this spec
425	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — interface header (issue 303)
426	- `issues/303-language-runtime-spec.md` — spec contract this implements
427	- `issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md` — dispatch layer that calls
428	  `invoke`
429	- `issues/302-wire-value-slot-store.md` — large-value heap
430	- `issues/222-compile-button-and-assets-directory.md` — where compiled
431	  `.so`s live (`compiled/bin/`)
432	- `issues/306-lua-language-spec.md` — sister spec; same shape, no
433	  compile step
434	- `maps/driver-test` — multi-language test map including C boxes
435	
436	## Implementation log
437	
438	### Compile + invoke — 2026-05-12
439	
440	`langs/c/spec.c`:
441	- `compile(src, out)` forks gcc with `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall`.
442	- `init` allocates a 32-entry dlopen cache; `teardown` walks it.
443	- `invoke` looks up the .so in the cache or dlopens it, dlsyms
444	  the named function, and calls it through a fixed signature:
445	  `int fn(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
446	  void *out_buf, int out_capacity, int *out_size)`.
447	
448	Convention: C box functions all match that signature. The
449	optional typed-wrapper mode (per-box signature parsing +
450	generated marshaller) is deferred until a box wants types that
451	diverge from the byte-array shape.
452	
453	`tests/maps/hello/src/echo.c` ships two fixture functions; five
454	tests in `tests/307-c-spec-test.c` compile and invoke them, plus
455	the missing-symbol and bad-source failure modes.
456	
```

Specs never know about other languages — adding a new language means implementing those two functions. No pair-wise paths (no \"lua → c\" code path); the language-agnostic JSON intermediate is non-negotiable.\n\n**JSON can carry shared-memory references.** A primitive like `{\"$ref\": {\"ptr\": 140234..., \"len\": 1048576, \"type\": \"bytes\"}}` references bytes living in shared heap. In-process specs (Lua, C) deref directly or copy per their language's needs (`lua_pushlstring` copies into Lua's heap; C reads at the pointer). Cross-process boundaries (anything ↔ Bash) inline the bytes at the UDS marshaller. The wire format itself is always JSON; `$ref` is just one primitive form.\n\n**No handles, no back-routing.** A \"handle that routes operations back to the owning spec\" was rejected — real library functions (qsort, libcurl, etc.) can't interpret SoraMech ids. Destination spec either has the means to interpret what arrived (e.g. Lua's spec uses LuaJIT FFI to wrap a `$function_pointer` primitive into a callable) or the wire compile fails. Specs are self-contained.\n\n**Type ambiguities resolved by wiring, not annotation.** The system never asks vocab questions like \"is param 2 the discriminator?\" or \"is this int the length?\" — it can't, because that vocabulary isn't universal. A C function `void foo(char *p, int n)` exposes two independent input slots; the user wires them according to the function's documentation. Same for unions (one slot for the variant bytes, one slot for the tag). The system never infers pairings; the user expresses them by wiring.\n\n**Spec interface (final shape):**\n- `to_json(native_value, output_signature) → json` — emits JSON with embedded `$ref` / `$function_pointer` / `$lang_opaque` primitives as needed.\n- `from_json(json, input_signature) → native_value` — reconstructs the value as the destination language's native form. May synthesize callables (libffi trampolines for C, `ffi.cast` for LuaJIT) when the destination's signature is a function-pointer type.\n- `list_functions(path)` and `infer_signature(path, fn)` — editor introspection. All function parameters exposed as separate slots; no inference about pairings.\n- plus existing `init`, `call`, `shutdown`.\n\n**Wire primitives in the JSON contract:**\n- `$ref` — pointer + length + type tag for shared-memory bytes. In-process only; marshaller inlines bytes at cross-process boundary.\n- `$function_pointer` — address + signature for callables. In-process only; wire compile fails into Bash.\n- `$lang_opaque` — tag saying \"these bytes are language-X-internal.\" Destination spec either knows what to do or wire compile fails. Used for things like Lua coroutines into C where C has no general way to interpret the bytes.\n\n**Why:** Three corrections drove the design:\n1. Handles were rejected because library functions in the wild can't be modified to use them.\n2. Pair-wise paths were rejected because every new language would break the system.\n3. Per-function discriminator/length annotations were rejected because the system has no vocabulary to ask about them universally.\n\n**How to apply:** When discussing cross-language data passing, spec design, the wire format, or the JSON bridge: every wire carries JSON, period. Big values use `$ref` primitives that reference shared heap. Specs implement only the two pair-symmetric callbacks. Type ambiguities are user wiring decisions, not system prompts. The natural home for this design in the issue tree is issue 317; the C-side parser/wrapper work is issue 307.\n",

{
  "content":"---\nname: Cross-language design pitfalls\ndescription: Three approaches the user rejected when designing SoraMech's cross-language wire format\ntype: feedback\n---\n\nWhen designing anything that touches the cross-language data path, avoid these three shapes — the user pushed back on each and the rejections are load-bearing for the architecture.\n\n**1. Don't propose runtime \"handles\" that route operations back to the originating spec.**\n\n**Why:** Real library functions in the wild (qsort, libcurl, anything from libc) expect actual native values — a real `int (*)(int,int)` function pointer, a real `char *` — not a SoraMech-issued id string. They can't be modified to participate in our runtime's routing scheme. The wire must carry data the destination spec can fully reconstruct into a native value, not opaque tokens.\n\n**How to apply:** When a value seems hard to serialize (function pointers, coroutines, opaque pointers), decompose to data primitives (address + signature, bytecode + upvalues, ptr + length). If the destination needs a callable, the destination spec synthesizes one at reconstruction time (libffi trampoline for C, `ffi.cast` for LuaJIT). The \"phone home\" callback that the trampoline performs internally is fine — it's an artifact of the destination spec's reconstruction, not exposed in the wire format. What's forbidden is a wire primitive that says \"operations on this value cross back to the source.\"\n\n**2. Don't propose pair-wise language conversion paths (lua→c, c→lua, etc.).**\n\n**Why:** N languages means N² conversion paths, which means adding a new language breaks the existing ones. The wire must use a language-agnostic intermediate (JSON) and every spec must implement only the two pair-symmetric callbacks (`to_json`, `from_json`). New language = implement those two functions, nothing else touches.\n\n**How to apply:** Never write code that names two specific languages in a conversion context. Specs only ever see JSON on the wire. If a JSON primitive needs an extension (like `$function_pointer`), it lives in the universal JSON contract, not as a lua-spec-specific or c-spec-specific concept.\n\n**3. Don't propose per-function annotations for type ambiguities (lengths, discriminators, callback signatures).**\n\n**Why:** The user pointed out that \"discriminator\" is a vocab word the system has no general way to ask about. We can't prompt \"which param is the length?\" because that pairing convention lives in the function's English documentation, not its types. Asking the user to write annotations means asking them to know specialized vocabulary they didn't sign up for.\n\n**How to apply:** When a function signature has ambiguous types (`char *` with no length, `union` with no tag, `void *` with no signature), expose each parameter as a separate input slot on the box. The user wires the slots according to the function's documentation — `p` from one source, `n` from another. The system never infers pairings; the user expresses them by wiring. If the destination needs a length and no length wire is provided, the wire compile fails with a plain-English fix-it message (\"destination needs length, source provides only a pointer — wire a length input here\").\n\n**Common thread:** all three rejections are the same principle in different clothes — **SoraMech bends to the language, not the other way around.** Users write idiomatic code in their language. Libraries are used as-is. Specs are self-contained and pair-symmetric. The system stays out of the user's vocabulary.\n",
  "file_path":"/home/ritz/.claude/projects/-mnt-mtwo-programs-sora-soramech/memory/feedback_cross_language_pitfalls.md"
}

_tool_result:_
```
/* langs/lang-spec.h — public interface for SoraMech language specs.
 *
 * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language
 * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool
 * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at
 * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.
 *
 * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()
 * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;
 * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the
 * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source
 * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.
 *
 * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch
 * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,
 * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.
 *
 * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path
 * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised
 * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification
 * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are
 * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them
 * fall back to `invoke` for every call.
 */

#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H

#include <stddef.h>

#ifdef __cplusplus
extern "C" {
#endif

/* {{{ Shared signatures */
/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and
 * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based
 * on the wire-format classification in issue 312. */
typedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,
                              const char *file_path,
                              const char *fn_name,
                              const void **input_data,
                              const int   *input_sizes,
                              int          n_inputs,
                              void        *out_buf,
                              int          out_buf_capacity,
                              int         *out_size);

/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default). */
typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(const void *src, int src_size,
                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
/* }}} */

/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
typedef struct lang_spec {
    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
    const char *file_ext;   /* ".lua", ".sh", ".c", ".py"        */

    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an
     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch
     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */
    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);

    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
     * persistent socket connections). */
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);

    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path. Returns 0 on
     * success, nonzero on failure. */
    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path);

    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
     *
     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
     * paths below are not set. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke;

    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;

    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
```

3	 * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language
4	 * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool
5	 * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at
6	 * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.
7	 *
8	 * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()
9	 * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;
10	 * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the
11	 * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source
12	 * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.
13	 *
14	 * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch
15	 * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,
16	 * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.
17	 *
18	 * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path
19	 * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised
20	 * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification
21	 * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are
22	 * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them
23	 * fall back to `invoke` for every call.
24	 */
25	
26	#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
27	#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
28	
29	#include <stddef.h>
30	
31	#ifdef __cplusplus
32	extern "C" {
33	#endif
34	
35	/* {{{ Shared signatures */
36	/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and
37	 * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based
38	 * on the wire-format classification in issue 312. */
39	typedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,
40	                              const char *file_path,
41	                              const char *fn_name,
42	                              const void **input_data,
43	                              const int   *input_sizes,
44	                              int          n_inputs,
45	                              void        *out_buf,
46	                              int          out_buf_capacity,
47	                              int         *out_size);
48	
49	/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
50	 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
51	 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
52	 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default). */
53	typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(const void *src, int src_size,
54	                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
55	/* }}} */
56	
57	/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
58	typedef struct lang_spec {
59	    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
60	    const char *file_ext;   /* ".lua", ".sh", ".c", ".py"        */
61	
62	    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an
63	     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch
64	     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */
65	    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
66	
67	    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
68	     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
69	     * persistent socket connections). */
70	    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
71	
72	    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
73	     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
74	     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
75	     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path. Returns 0 on
76	     * success, nonzero on failure. */
77	    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path);
78	
79	    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
80	     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
81	     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
82	     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
83	     *
84	     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
85	     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
86	     * paths below are not set. */
87	    lang_invoke_fn invoke;
88	
89	    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
90	     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
91	     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
92	     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
93	     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
94	     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
95	     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
96	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;
97	
98	    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
99	     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
100	     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
101	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;
102	
103	    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a
104	     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes
105	     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's
106	     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's
107	     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
108	     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue
109	     * 312. */
110	    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;
111	    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;
112	} lang_spec_t;
113	/* }}} */
114	
115	#ifdef __cplusplus
116	}
117	#endif
118	
119	#endif /* SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H */
120	
```

1	/* langs/lua/spec.c — Lua language spec for SoraMech.
2	 *
3	 * Per-worker handle is a `lua_State` with the standard libraries
4	 * opened. Invocation convention: the box's source file is expected
5	 * to return a Lua table (`return M` at the bottom), and the
6	 * fn_name is a key into that table. Inputs arrive as byte arrays;
7	 * each one is pushed as a Lua string. The function's single return
8	 * value is converted to a string (via tostring semantics) and
9	 * memcpy'd into the caller's output buffer.
10	 *
11	 * Designed in issue 306. Implementation choices that matter:
12	 *
13	 *  - One lua_State per worker thread. lua_State is not
14	 *    thread-safe, but per-thread requires no locking.
15	 *  - The state's stack is rewound to its baseline depth at every
16	 *    invoke entry/exit so cross-call leaks don't accumulate.
17	 *  - File caching (luaL_loadfile once, replayed via lua_call) is
18	 *    deferred — this iteration loads the chunk on every invoke.
19	 *    Lands as a follow-on once profiling justifies it.
20	 *  - The chunk's side effects run on every invoke too (since
21	 *    we re-load and re-execute). Box source files should be pure
22	 *    "return M" modules for now.
23	 */
24	
25	#include "lang-spec.h"
26	
27	#include <lua.h>
28	#include <lauxlib.h>
29	#include <lualib.h>
30	
31	#include <stdio.h>
32	#include <string.h>
33	
34	/* Registry key for the per-worker file → module-table cache. The
35	 * key is a string in the Lua registry; the value is a Lua table
36	 * keyed by file path. Each entry's value is the module table the
37	 * file's chunk returned.
38	 *
39	 * Caching is per-`lua_State` (i.e. per-worker), which matches the
40	 * thread model: each worker has its own state, never shared. No
41	 * locking needed because only the worker that owns the state ever
42	 * touches the cache. */
43	#define LUA_CACHE_KEY "soramech.module_cache"
44	
45	/* {{{ lua_init() — create a state, open standard libs, seed cache */
46	static void *lua_init(int worker_idx)
47	{
48	    (void)worker_idx;
49	    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
50	    if (!L) return NULL;
51	    luaL_openlibs(L);
52	    /* Seed the empty cache table. */
53	    lua_newtable(L);
54	    lua_setfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
55	    return L;
56	}
57	/* }}} */
58	
59	/* {{{ lua_teardown() */
60	static void lua_teardown(void *handle)
61	{
62	    if (handle) lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
63	}
64	/* }}} */
65	
66	/* {{{ lua_invoke() */
67	static int lua_invoke(void *handle,
68	                      const char  *file_path,
69	                      const char  *fn_name,
70	                      const void **input_data,
71	                      const int   *input_sizes,
72	                      int          n_inputs,
73	                      void        *out_buf,
74	                      int          out_buf_capacity,
75	                      int         *out_size)
76	{
77	    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;
78	    if (!L || !file_path || !fn_name) return -1;
79	
80	    int baseline = lua_gettop(L);
81	
82	    /* Module cache lookup. The cache table lives in the registry
83	     * under LUA_CACHE_KEY; each key is a file path, each value is
84	     * the module table the file's chunk returned. Stack after this
85	     * block: ..., cache, module. */
86	    lua_getfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
87	    lua_getfield(L, -1, file_path);
88	    if (lua_isnil(L, -1)) {
89	        lua_pop(L, 1);   /* discard nil */
90	        /* Stack: ..., cache */
91	
92	        if (luaL_loadfile(L, file_path) != 0) {
93	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: loadfile('%s'): %s\n",
94	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
95	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
96	            return -1;
97	        }
98	        if (lua_pcall(L, 0, 1, 0) != 0) {
99	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: chunk exec '%s': %s\n",
100	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
101	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
102	            return -1;
103	        }
104	        if (!lua_istable(L, -1)) {
105	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' did not return a table\n", file_path);
106	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
107	            return -1;
108	        }
109	        /* Stack: ..., cache, module. Cache the module under
110	         * file_path. */
111	        lua_pushvalue(L, -1);
112	        lua_setfield(L, -3, file_path);
113	    }
114	    /* Stack at this point: ..., cache, module. */
115	
116	    /* Pull the function out of the module table. */
117	    lua_getfield(L, -1, fn_name);
118	    if (!lua_isfunction(L, -1)) {
119	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' is not a function in %s\n",
120	                fn_name, file_path);
121	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
122	        return -1;
123	    }
124	    /* Stack: ..., cache, module, function. Drop module + cache. */
125	    lua_remove(L, -2);   /* drop module */
126	    lua_remove(L, -2);   /* drop cache  */
127	
128	    /* Push each input as a Lua string. */
129	    for (int i = 0; i < n_inputs; i++) {
130	        if (input_data[i] == NULL && input_sizes[i] > 0) {
131	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: input %d is NULL with size %d\n",
132	                    i, input_sizes[i]);
133	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
134	            return -1;
135	        }
136	        lua_pushlstring(L, (const char *)input_data[i], (size_t)input_sizes[i]);
137	    }
138	
139	    /* Call. One return value expected. */
140	    if (lua_pcall(L, n_inputs, 1, 0) != 0) {
141	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: %s.%s: %s\n",
142	                file_path, fn_name, lua_tostring(L, -1));
143	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
144	        return -1;
145	    }
146	
147	    /* Convert the return to a string. tostring handles numbers /
148	     * booleans / nil; if the spec needs richer marshaling later
149	     * (tables, structs), that's an issue-312-shaped change. */
150	    size_t len = 0;
151	    const char *result = lua_tolstring(L, -1, &len);
152	    if (!result) {
153	        /* Non-coercible value. For now, surface as zero-length
154	         * output rather than an error — the box returned something
155	         * we can't serialize, but the call itself succeeded. */
156	        if (out_size) *out_size = 0;
157	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
158	        return 0;
159	    }
160	    if ((int)len > out_buf_capacity) {
161	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: %s.%s returned %zu bytes; "
162	                        "buffer is %d\n",
163	                file_path, fn_name, len, out_buf_capacity);
164	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
165	        return -1;
166	    }
167	    if (out_buf && len > 0) memcpy(out_buf, result, len);
168	    if (out_size) *out_size = (int)len;
169	
170	    lua_settop(L, baseline);
171	    return 0;
172	}
173	/* }}} */
174	
175	/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */
176	lang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {
177	    .name     = "lua",
178	    .file_ext = ".lua",
179	    .init     = lua_init,
180	    .teardown = lua_teardown,
181	    .compile  = 0,
182	    .invoke   = lua_invoke,
183	};
184	/* }}} */
185	
```

1	#!/bin/bash
2	# scripts/run-tests.sh — phase 3 integration tests.
3	#
4	# Runs every fixture map under tests/maps/ through `soramech-pool`
5	# and asserts that the captured outputs match a hardcoded
6	# expectation per map. The point is to catch regressions
7	# end-to-end — past the spec interface, past the dispatch action,
8	# past JSONL writing — rather than via the in-process unit tests.
9	#
10	# Per the project convention: scripts run from any directory via a
11	# hard-coded ${DIR} path with an override argument; all paths are
12	# relative to ${DIR}.
13	
14	set -u
15	
16	DIR="/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech"
17	if [[ $# -ge 1 ]]; then
18	    DIR="$1"
19	fi
20	
21	cd "$DIR"
22	
23	pass=0
24	fail=0
25	failures=()
26	
27	# {{{ check_map() — run one fixture, verify expected substrings
28	check_map() {
29	    local map="$1"
30	    shift
31	    local expected=("$@")
32	
33	    printf "  %-44s " "$map"
34	
35	    local output
36	    output=$("$DIR/soramech-pool" "$DIR/tests/maps/$map" 2>&1)
37	    local rc=$?
38	
39	    local missing=""
40	    if [[ $rc -ne 0 ]]; then
41	        missing="exit=$rc"
42	    fi
43	    for e in "${expected[@]}"; do
44	        if [[ "$output" != *"$e"* ]]; then
45	            missing+="${missing:+; }missing '$e'"
46	        fi
47	    done
48	
49	    if [[ -z "$missing" ]]; then
50	        printf "ok\n"
51	        pass=$((pass + 1))
52	    else
53	        printf "FAIL — %s\n" "$missing"
54	        fail=$((fail + 1))
55	        failures+=("$map: $missing")
56	    fi
57	}
58	# }}}
59	
60	# {{{ pipeline_output_check() — additional: the pipeline writes to disk
61	pipeline_output_check() {
62	    local file="/tmp/soramech-pipeline-out.txt"
63	    rm -f "$file"
64	    "$DIR/soramech-pool" "$DIR/tests/maps/pipeline" > /dev/null 2>&1
65	    printf "  %-44s " "pipeline file_write output"
66	    if [[ -f "$file" ]]; then
67	        local content
68	        content=$(cat "$file")
69	        if [[ "$content" == "11!" ]]; then
70	            printf "ok\n"
71	            pass=$((pass + 1))
72	        else
73	            printf "FAIL — expected '11!', got '%s'\n" "$content"
74	            fail=$((fail + 1))
75	            failures+=("pipeline file: $content")
76	        fi
77	    else
78	        printf "FAIL — output file missing\n"
79	        fail=$((fail + 1))
80	        failures+=("pipeline: $file missing")
81	    fi
82	}
83	# }}}
84	
85	# {{{ compile_pipeline_check() — soramech-compile produces a portable artifact
86	# Verifies issue 309's compile pipeline: bundles all spec.so + sources
87	# into compiled/, then runs the artifact from /tmp (i.e. with no
88	# dependency on the project's cwd) and verifies it produces the same
89	# output as the source map.
90	compile_pipeline_check() {
91	    local map="$DIR/tests/maps/pipeline"
92	    local compiled="$map/compiled"
93	    local file="/tmp/soramech-pipeline-out.txt"
94	
95	    printf "  %-44s " "compile pipeline (portable run)"
96	
97	    rm -rf "$compiled"
98	    if ! "$DIR/scripts/soramech-compile.sh" "$map" --dir "$DIR" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
99	        printf "FAIL — soramech-compile failed\n"
100	        fail=$((fail + 1))
101	        failures+=("compile: script returned non-zero")
102	        return
103	    fi
104	    if [[ ! -x "$compiled/pool-runner" ]]; then
105	        printf "FAIL — compiled/pool-runner missing or not executable\n"
106	        fail=$((fail + 1))
107	        failures+=("compile: pool-runner missing")
108	        return
109	    fi
110	
111	    rm -f "$file"
112	    local output
113	    output=$(cd /tmp && "$compiled/pool-runner" "$compiled" 2>&1)
114	
115	    local content=""
116	    [[ -f "$file" ]] && content=$(cat "$file")
117	
118	    if [[ "$output" == *"addone → 11"* ]] && [[ "$content" == "11!" ]]; then
119	        printf "ok\n"
120	        pass=$((pass + 1))
121	    else
122	        printf "FAIL — output='%s' file='%s'\n" "$output" "$content"
123	        fail=$((fail + 1))
124	        failures+=("compile: bad output")
125	    fi
126	}
127	# }}}
128	
129	# {{{ run all fixtures
130	echo "integration tests (fixture maps):"
131	
132	check_map "calc" \
133	    "add → 42"
134	
135	check_map "hello" \
136	    "who → World" \
137	    "greet → Hello, World!"
138	
139	check_map "comparator" \
140	    "classify → 8" \
141	    "high → HIGH:8" \
142	    "low → (no output)" \
143	    "mid → (no output)"
144	
145	check_map "iter-route" \
146	    "iter → hi" \
147	    "a → A:hi" \
148	    "b → (no output)" \
149	    "c → (no output)"
150	
151	check_map "pipeline" \
152	    "input → 5" \
153	    "double → 10" \
154	    "addone → 11" \
155	    "shout → 11!"
156	
157	pipeline_output_check
158	compile_pipeline_check
159	# }}}
160	
161	# {{{ parser_tests() — issue 232: unit tests for langs/<lang>/parser.js
162	# Run Node's built-in test runner over each per-language parser test
163	# file and fold the result into the surrounding pass/fail counters.
164	# Each .mjs file is one logical "test" from this script's perspective;
165	# Node prints its own subtest breakdown on failure for the diagnostic.
166	parser_tests() {
167	    if ! command -v node >/dev/null 2>&1; then
168	        printf "  %-44s SKIP — node not installed\n" "parser tests (lua, bash)"
169	        return
170	    fi
171	    for tf in "$DIR/tests/232-lua-parser-test.mjs" \
172	              "$DIR/tests/233-bash-parser-test.mjs" \
173	              "$DIR/tests/234-language-spec-js-test.mjs"; do
174	        local label
175	        label=$(basename "$tf")
176	        printf "  %-44s " "$label"
177	        local out
178	        out=$(SORAMECH_DIR="$DIR" node --test "$tf" 2>&1)
179	        local rc=$?
180	        if [[ $rc -eq 0 ]]; then
181	            printf "ok\n"
182	            pass=$((pass + 1))
183	        else
184	            printf "FAIL\n"
185	            echo "$out" | sed 's/^/      /'
186	            fail=$((fail + 1))
187	            failures+=("$label: node --test exit=$rc")
188	        fi
189	    done
190	}
191	parser_tests
192	# }}}
193	
194	echo
195	echo "  $pass passed, $fail failed"
196	
197	if [[ $fail -gt 0 ]]; then
198	    echo
199	    echo "  failures:"
200	    for f in "${failures[@]}"; do
201	        echo "    - $f"
202	    done
203	fi
204	
205	exit $fail
206	
```

# langs/lua/Makefile — build the Lua language spec.
#
# Produces spec.so, a small .so the pool runner dlopens at startup.
# Links against LuaJIT 2.1 (override LUA_INCLUDE / LUA_LIB if the
# headers and library live somewhere other than the Debian/Arch
# default).
#
# Invokable standalone (`make -C langs/lua`) or from the top-level
# Makefile via `make specs`. The DIR variable lets the top-level
# pass the project root so the -I path to langs/lang-spec.h resolves.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST)))/../..)
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC          ?= gcc
LUA_INCLUDE ?= /usr/include/luajit-2.1
LUA_LIB     ?= /usr/lib

CFLAGS  = -shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall -I$(LUA_INCLUDE) -I$(DIR)/langs
LDFLAGS = -L$(LUA_LIB) -lluajit-5.1
# }}}

# {{{ Build rules
.PHONY: all clean

all: spec.so

spec.so: spec.c $(DIR)/langs/lang-spec.h
	@$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -o $@ $< $(LDFLAGS)

clean:
	@rm -f spec.so
# }}}
```

1	/* libs/json/json.h — small DOM JSON parser + streaming writer.
2	 *
3	 * Written for SoraMech, not vendored. Designed in issue 314. Two
4	 * halves: a DOM parser with arena-allocated nodes (used by the
5	 * phase 3 graph loader, issue 305), and a streaming writer with a
6	 * fixed buffer (used by the JSONL run-log writer, issue 311).
7	 *
8	 * The parser is hand-rolled recursive descent. The DOM is read-only
9	 * once parsed; constructing JSON goes through the writer, not the
10	 * DOM. Numbers are doubles; integer-shaped fields read via
11	 * (int)json_number_value(...). UTF-8 strict, BMP \uXXXX with
12	 * surrogate-pair handling. No comments, no trailing commas.
13	 *
14	 * Errors: json_parse returns NULL on failure, with *err_offset
15	 * and *err_msg filled. The graph loader maps the offset to a
16	 * filename:line:column diagnostic before surfacing it.
17	 *
18	 * Memory: every parse tree lives in one json_arena_t. Destroying
19	 * the arena frees every node and every string in one call. There
20	 * is no per-node free.
21	 *
22	 * Threading: single-threaded — the parser is meant to run on the
23	 * graph-load thread before the pool spins up. Concurrency comes in
24	 * at the writer side, where each writer call is independent and the
25	 * caller owns the buffer.
26	 */
27

29	#define SORAMECH_JSON_H
30	
31	#include <stddef.h>
32	
33	#ifdef __cplusplus

35	#endif

41	    JSON_NUMBER,
42	    JSON_STRING,
43	    JSON_ARRAY,
44	    JSON_OBJECT,
45	} json_kind_t;
46	
47	typedef struct json_arena json_arena_t;
48	typedef struct json_node  json_node_t;
49	/* }}} */
50	
51	/* {{{ Arena lifecycle */
52	/* Construct an empty arena. Returns NULL on allocation failure. */
53	json_arena_t *json_arena_create(void);
54	
55	/* Free every chunk in the arena. Safe on NULL. */
56	void          json_arena_destroy(json_arena_t *a);
57	
58	/* Bytes currently allocated across all chunks (for diagnostics). */
59	size_t        json_arena_bytes(const json_arena_t *a);
60	/* }}} */
61	
62	/* {{{ Parser */
63	/* Parse a NUL-terminated UTF-8 source into a tree owned by the
64	 * arena. Returns NULL on syntax error; *err_offset (if non-NULL)
65	 * receives the byte offset where parsing failed, and *err_msg
66	 * (if non-NULL) receives a static-string description. */
67	json_node_t *json_parse(json_arena_t *a,
68	                        const char  *src,
69	                        int         *err_offset,
70	                        const char **err_msg);
71	
72	/* Read a file end-to-end and parse it. *err_line (if non-NULL) is
73	 * the 1-based line number where parsing failed, computed from the
74	 * byte offset against the file contents. */
75	json_node_t *json_parse_file(json_arena_t *a,
76	                             const char  *path,
77	                             int         *err_line,
78	                             const char **err_msg);
79	/* }}} */
80	
81	/* {{{ Accessors */
82	json_kind_t   json_kind        (const json_node_t *n);
83	int           json_is_null     (const json_node_t *n);
84	int           json_bool_value  (const json_node_t *n);
85	double        json_number_value(const json_node_t *n);

# SoraMech — top-level build orchestration.
#
# What it does, in a sentence: builds the phase 3 pool runner binary
# plus one shared library per language spec. Release by default;
# DEBUG=1 keeps symbols and disables optimization, STRICT=1 promotes
# warnings to errors. `make clean` removes everything that was built.
#
# Invokable from any directory: `make -C /path/to/soramech` works,
# and the DIR variable can be overridden if needed (e.g. for an
# out-of-tree checkout). All paths in this Makefile are relative to
# $(DIR) per the project convention.
#
# Designed in issue 309. Implementation of the runner's runtime is
# split across issues 301–308; this Makefile builds whatever sources
# exist under src/, libs/task-pool/, and libs/json/, so it scales as
# those issues land.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST))))
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC      ?= gcc

# Shared header search path: every C source can find langs/lang-spec.h
# and any future libs/<name>/ headers via -I flags below.
CPPFLAGS = -I$(DIR)/langs -I$(DIR)/libs/task-pool -I$(DIR)/libs/json -I$(DIR)/src

# Default to release; DEBUG and STRICT toggle alternates.
ifeq ($(DEBUG),1)
  CFLAGS = -O0 -g -DDEBUG -Wall -pthread -fPIC
else ifeq ($(STRICT),1)
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic -Werror -pthread -fPIC
else
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -pthread -fPIC
endif

LDFLAGS = -ldl -pthread
# }}}

# {{{ Paths and source discovery
BUILD_DIR = $(DIR)/build
POOL_BIN  = $(DIR)/soramech-pool

# Wildcards: empty subdirs return empty lists, which is fine. As
# issues 301/302/303/304/305 land their C sources here, they get
# compiled automatically — no Makefile edit required.
POOL_SOURCES = \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/src/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/task-pool/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/json/*.c)

POOL_OBJECTS = $(POOL_SOURCES:$(DIR)/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/%.o)

# Test sources live in tests/ as C files named to match the source
# they exercise (e.g. tests/009-slot-store-test.c exercises
# src/009-slot-store.c). Each builds to a separate executable that
# `make test` runs in turn.
TEST_SOURCES = $(wildcard $(DIR)/tests/*.c)
TEST_BINS    = $(TEST_SOURCES:$(DIR)/tests/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/tests/%)

LANGS = lua c bash
# }}}

# {{{ Public targets
.PHONY: all runner specs clean test help

all: runner specs

runner: $(POOL_BIN)

specs:
	@for lang in $(LANGS); do \
	  echo "  → langs/$$lang"; \
	  $(MAKE) -s -C $(DIR)/langs/$$lang DIR=$(DIR) || exit 1; \
	done

clean:
	rm -f $(POOL_BIN)
	rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)
```

   - Language-agnostic specs (Lua spec must not know ANYTHING about C or bash)

   - Shared memory utilization for the threading model
   - Per-spec rules, not per-map
   - JSON-always wire format for N-language decoupling
   - No "handles" that route operations back to source language

Hey. Picking back up — we were about to start implementing the Lua spec's JSON bridges (`native_to_json` / `json_to_native`).

Before I touch code, one thing to confirm: the bridge signature in `langs/lang-spec.h:53` is

```c
typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(const void *src, int src_size,
                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
```

That's missing the worker handle (the `lua_State *` for the Lua spec). To encode/decode a Lua value, the bridge needs access to the state — either to read the value off the top of the stack (encode) or push the parsed value to the top (decode). The convention from issue 317 was: top-of-stack is the protocol.

I want to add `void *handle` as the first parameter. No callers exist yet (only the typedef + the NULL slots in each spec struct), so it's a safe change. Sound good, or did you want a different shape — e.g. pass the JSON bytes through stdin/stdout-ish buffers and let the spec keep its own state-juggling internal?

_tool_result:_
```
json.c
json.h
json.info.md
# SoraMech — top-level build orchestration.
#
# What it does, in a sentence: builds the phase 3 pool runner binary
# plus one shared library per language spec. Release by default;
# DEBUG=1 keeps symbols and disables optimization, STRICT=1 promotes
# warnings to errors. `make clean` removes everything that was built.
#
# Invokable from any directory: `make -C /path/to/soramech` works,
# and the DIR variable can be overridden if needed (e.g. for an
# out-of-tree checkout). All paths in this Makefile are relative to
# $(DIR) per the project convention.
#
# Designed in issue 309. Implementation of the runner's runtime is
# split across issues 301–308; this Makefile builds whatever sources
# exist under src/, libs/task-pool/, and libs/json/, so it scales as
# those issues land.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST))))
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC      ?= gcc

# Shared header search path: every C source can find langs/lang-spec.h
# and any future libs/<name>/ headers via -I flags below.
CPPFLAGS = -I$(DIR)/langs -I$(DIR)/libs/task-pool -I$(DIR)/libs/json -I$(DIR)/src

# Default to release; DEBUG and STRICT toggle alternates.
ifeq ($(DEBUG),1)
  CFLAGS = -O0 -g -DDEBUG -Wall -pthread -fPIC
else ifeq ($(STRICT),1)
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic -Werror -pthread -fPIC
else
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -pthread -fPIC
endif

LDFLAGS = -ldl -pthread
# }}}

# {{{ Paths and source discovery
BUILD_DIR = $(DIR)/build
POOL_BIN  = $(DIR)/soramech-pool

# Wildcards: empty subdirs return empty lists, which is fine. As
# issues 301/302/303/304/305 land their C sources here, they get
# compiled automatically — no Makefile edit required.
POOL_SOURCES = \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/src/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/task-pool/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/json/*.c)

POOL_OBJECTS = $(POOL_SOURCES:$(DIR)/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/%.o)

# Test sources live in tests/ as C files named to match the source
# they exercise (e.g. tests/009-slot-store-test.c exercises
# src/009-slot-store.c). Each builds to a separate executable that
# `make test` runs in turn.
TEST_SOURCES = $(wildcard $(DIR)/tests/*.c)
TEST_BINS    = $(TEST_SOURCES:$(DIR)/tests/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/tests/%)

LANGS = lua c bash
# }}}

# {{{ Public targets
.PHONY: all runner specs clean test help

all: runner specs

runner: $(POOL_BIN)

specs:
	@for lang in $(LANGS); do \
	  echo "  → langs/$$lang"; \
	  $(MAKE) -s -C $(DIR)/langs/$$lang DIR=$(DIR) || exit 1; \
	done

clean:
	rm -f $(POOL_BIN)
	rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)
```

$(BUILD_DIR)/tests/314-json-test:         $(BUILD_DIR)/libs/json/json.o

# Pattern: link a test binary. $^ collects the .o files declared

{
  "new_string":"/* {{{ encode_value() — write one Lua value at `abs_idx` as JSON */\n/* Walk the value at absolute stack index `abs_idx` and emit JSON\n * via the streaming writer `w`. Recurses into tables. Stack-neutral:\n * the value at abs_idx stays put (we only push/pop scratch keys\n * during table traversal). Returns 0 ok, -1 on unsupported type or\n * writer truncation. The \"unsupported type\" path is loud — function,\n * userdata, thread, lightuserdata produce a stderr error rather\n * than a silent fallback (project convention forbids those). The\n * richer primitives — $ref, $function_pointer, $lang_opaque — land\n * later; this first cut only handles the JSON-clean scalars and\n * containers. */\nstatic int encode_value(lua_State *L, int abs_idx, json_writer_t *w)\n{\n    int t = lua_type(L, abs_idx);\n    switch (t) {\n    case LUA_TNIL:\n        json_writer_null(w);\n        return 0;\n    case LUA_TBOOLEAN:\n        json_writer_bool(w, lua_toboolean(L, abs_idx));\n        return 0;\n    case LUA_TNUMBER: {\n        /* Integer-shaped numbers go via writer_int so JSON doesn't\n         * show \"3.0\" where \"3\" reads better. The float path uses\n         * %g internally inside the writer. */\n        lua_Number n = lua_tonumber(L, abs_idx);\n        long long  i = (long long)n;\n        if ((lua_Number)i == n) json_writer_int(w, i);\n        else                    json_writer_number(w, (double)n);\n        return 0;\n    }\n    case LUA_TSTRING:\n        json_writer_string(w, lua_tostring(L, abs_idx));\n        return 0;\n    case LUA_TTABLE: {\n        /* Array vs object: we treat a table as an array when its\n         * # length is positive AND a full key walk turns up exactly\n         * that many entries (no holes, no extra string keys). Empty\n         * tables encode as {} — a deliberate choice; JSON's [] vs {}\n         * distinction has no Lua equivalent and the object form\n         * keeps round-trips honest when downstream code expects a\n         * map. Future: an explicit array marker (issue 317 follow-up)\n         * if this becomes a footgun. */\n        int n = (int)lua_objlen(L, abs_idx);\n        int total = 0;\n        lua_pushnil(L);\n        while (lua_next(L, abs_idx) != 0) {\n            total++;\n            lua_pop(L, 1);\n        }\n        int is_array = (n > 0 && total == n);\n\n        if (is_array) {\n            json_writer_array(w);\n            for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {\n                lua_rawgeti(L, abs_idx, i);\n                int top = lua_gettop(L);\n                int r = encode_value(L, top, w);\n                lua_pop(L, 1);\n                if (r != 0) return -1;\n            }\n            json_writer_end(w);\n        } else {\n            json_writer_object(w);\n            lua_pushnil(L);\n            while (lua_next(L, abs_idx) != 0) {\n                /* Key at -2, value at -1. We must NOT call\n                 * lua_tostring on the key in-place because that\n                 * would coerce numbers to strings and corrupt\n                 * lua_next's iteration state — render numeric keys\n                 * into a local buffer instead. */\n                int kt = lua_type(L, -2);\n                if (kt == LUA_TSTRING) {\n                    json_writer_key(w, lua_tostring(L, -2));\n                } else if (kt == LUA_TNUMBER) {\n                    char keybuf[32];\n                    lua_Number kn = lua_tonumber(L, -2);\n                    long long  ki = (long long)kn;\n                    if ((lua_Number)ki == kn) snprintf(keybuf, sizeof(keybuf), \"%lld\", ki);\n                    else                      snprintf(keybuf, sizeof(keybuf), \"%g\", (double)kn);\n                    json_writer_key(w, keybuf);\n                } else {\n                    fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: table key of type %s is not JSON-encodable\\n\",\n                            lua_typename(L, kt));\n                    lua_pop(L, 2);\n                    return -1;\n                }\n                int top = lua_gettop(L);   /* points at the value */\n                int r = encode_value(L, top, w);\n                lua_pop(L, 1);             /* drop value, keep key for lua_next */\n                if (r != 0) {\n                    lua_pop(L, 1);          /* drop the key too */\n                    return -1;\n                }\n            }\n            json_writer_end(w);\n        }\n        return 0;\n    }\n    default:\n        /* Function / userdata / thread / lightuserdata. The richer\n         * wire primitives ($function_pointer, $lang_opaque) decompose\n         * these into data the destination can rebuild, but that\n         * decomposition lands as a follow-on. Today: hard error. */\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: cannot JSON-encode value of type %s\\n\",\n                lua_typename(L, t));\n        return -1;\n    }\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ lua_native_to_json() — top-of-stack Lua value → JSON bytes */\n/* Per the bridge stack-protocol convention (lang-spec.h): the\n * producer leaves its native value at the top of its working store.\n * For Lua, that's the top of `lua_State`. The bridge consumes it —\n * one pop on success, on failure the stack is left untouched so the\n * caller can inspect / log before tearing down. */\nstatic int lua_native_to_json(void *handle,\n                              const void *src, int src_size,\n                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)\n{\n    (void)src;\n    (void)src_size;\n    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;\n    if (!L || !dst || dst_capacity <= 0) return -1;\n    if (lua_gettop(L) < 1) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: native_to_json called with empty stack\\n\");\n        return -1;\n    }\n\n    json_writer_t w;\n    json_writer_init(&w, (char *)dst, dst_capacity);\n\n    int top = lua_gettop(L);\n    if (encode_value(L, top, &w) != 0) return -1;\n\n    int n = json_writer_finish(&w);\n    if (n < 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: native_to_json: output buffer (%d bytes) too small\\n\",\n                dst_capacity);\n        return -1;\n    }\n    if (dst_size) *dst_size = n;\n    lua_pop(L, 1);\n    return 0;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ decode_node() — push one JSON node onto the Lua stack */\n/* Recursive walk over the parsed DOM. Each call adds exactly one\n * value to the stack on success; on failure the stack is wound back\n * to where this call started so partial decodes don't pollute it. */\nstatic int decode_node(lua_State *L, const json_node_t *node)\n{\n    int baseline = lua_gettop(L);\n    switch (json_kind(node)) {\n    case JSON_NULL:\n        lua_pushnil(L);\n        return 0;\n    case JSON_BOOL:\n        lua_pushboolean(L, json_bool_value(node));\n        return 0;\n    case JSON_NUMBER:\n        lua_pushnumber(L, json_number_value(node));\n        return 0;\n    case JSON_STRING:\n        lua_pushstring(L, json_string_value(node));\n        return 0;\n    case JSON_ARRAY: {\n        int n = json_array_size(node);\n        lua_createtable(L, n, 0);\n        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {\n            if (decode_node(L, json_array_at(node, i)) != 0) {\n                lua_settop(L, baseline);\n                return -1;\n            }\n            lua_rawseti(L, -2, i + 1);\n        }\n        return 0;\n    }\n    case JSON_OBJECT: {\n        int n = json_object_size(node);\n        lua_createtable(L, 0, n);\n        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {\n            if (decode_node(L, json_object_value(node, i)) != 0) {\n                lua_settop(L, baseline);\n                return -1;\n            }\n            lua_setfield(L, -2, json_object_key(node, i));\n        }\n        return 0;\n    }\n    }\n    fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: decode_node: unknown JSON kind\\n\");\n    lua_settop(L, baseline);\n    return -1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ lua_json_to_native() — JSON bytes → push value onto Lua stack */\n/* The bridge parses `src` into a transient arena, walks the DOM\n * onto the lua_State stack, then frees the arena. The arena is\n * created and destroyed inside the call — no per-bridge cache yet.\n * If parse-time perf shows up in profiles, we can stash an arena\n * inside the worker handle later.\n *\n * `src` may not be NUL-terminated (the caller passes us a slice\n * out of a wire buffer), so we copy into a local NUL-terminated\n * scratch before parsing — json_parse requires NUL termination\n * (see libs/json/json.h). */\nstatic int lua_json_to_native(void *handle,\n                              const void *src, int src_size,\n                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size)\n{\n    (void)dst;\n    (void)dst_capacity;\n    (void)dst_size;\n    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;\n    if (!L || !src || src_size <= 0) return -1;\n\n    char *scratch = (char *)malloc((size_t)src_size + 1);\n    if (!scratch) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: json_to_native: out of memory\\n\");\n        return -1;\n    }\n    memcpy(scratch, src, (size_t)src_size);\n    scratch[src_size] = '\\0';\n\n    json_arena_t *arena = json_arena_create();\n    if (!arena) {\n        free(scratch);\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: json_to_native: arena allocation failed\\n\");\n        return -1;\n    }\n\n    int err_offset = 0;\n    const char *err_msg = NULL;\n    json_node_t *node = json_parse(arena, scratch, &err_offset, &err_msg);\n    free(scratch);\n    if (!node) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec: json_to_native: parse error at byte %d: %s\\n\",\n                err_offset, err_msg ? err_msg : \"(no message)\");\n        json_arena_destroy(arena);\n        return -1;\n    }\n\n    int r = decode_node(L, node);\n    json_arena_destroy(arena);\n    return r;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */\nlang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {\n    .name           = \"lua\",\n    .file_ext       = \".lua\",\n    .init           = lua_init,\n    .teardown       = lua_teardown,\n    .compile        = 0,\n    .invoke         = lua_invoke,\n    .native_to_json = lua_native_to_json,\n    .json_to_native = lua_json_to_native,\n};\n/* }}} */",
  "replace_all":false,

  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/langs/lua/spec.c"

1	/* tests/306-lua-spec-test.c — end-to-end test of the Lua spec.
2	 *
3	 * Loads langs/lua/spec.so through the registry, runs init / invoke /
4	 * teardown against tests/maps/hello/src/hello.lua. Exercises the
5	 * file-load + module-table + named-function invocation path with
6	 * both positional and optional arguments.
7	 *
8	 * Run from the project root so `langs/` and `tests/maps/` are
9	 * reachable.
10	 */
11	
12	#include "011-spec-registry.h"
13	#include "lang-spec.h"
14	
15	#include <stdio.h>
16	#include <stdlib.h>
17	#include <string.h>
18	#include <sys/stat.h>
19	
20	/* {{{ Test harness */
21	static int g_pass = 0;
22	static int g_fail = 0;
23	
24	#define ASSERT(cond) \
25	    do { if (!(cond)) { \
26	        fprintf(stderr, "      %s:%d: %s\n", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond); \
27	        return 0; \
28	    } } while (0)
29	
30	#define RUN(name) \
31	    do { \
32	        fprintf(stdout, "  %-44s ", #name); fflush(stdout); \
33	        if (test_##name()) { fprintf(stdout, "ok\n"); g_pass++; } \
34	        else                { fprintf(stdout, "FAIL\n"); g_fail++; } \
35	    } while (0)
36	/* }}} */
37	
38	/* {{{ load_lua_spec() — shared by every test */
39	static const lang_spec_t *load_lua_spec(spec_registry_t **out_reg)
40	{
41	    char *err = NULL;
42	    spec_registry_t *r = spec_registry_load("langs", &err);
43	    if (!r) {
44	        fprintf(stderr, "registry load failed: %s\n", err ? err : "(null)");
45	        free(err);
46	        return NULL;
47	    }
48	    const lang_spec_t *lua = spec_registry_get(r, "lua");
49	    if (!lua || !lua->init || !lua->invoke || !lua->teardown) {
50	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec missing callbacks\n");
51	        spec_registry_destroy(r);
52	        return NULL;
53	    }
54	    *out_reg = r;
55	    return lua;
56	}
57	/* }}} */
58	
59	/* {{{ test_init_teardown() */
60	static int test_init_teardown(void)
61	{
62	    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;
63	    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);
64	    ASSERT(lua);
65	    void *h = lua->init(0);
66	    ASSERT(h);
67	    lua->teardown(h);
68	    spec_registry_destroy(r);
69	    return 1;
70	}
71	/* }}} */
72	
73	/* {{{ test_invoke_greet_both_args() */
74	static int test_invoke_greet_both_args(void)
75	{
76	    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;
77	    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);
78	    ASSERT(lua);
79	    void *h = lua->init(0);
80	    ASSERT(h);
81	
82	    const char *name = "World";
83	    const char *sal  = "Hi";
84	    const void *args[2]  = { name, sal };
85	    int         sizes[2] = { (int)strlen(name), (int)strlen(sal) };
86	    char buf[128];
87	    int n = 0;
88	
89	    int rc = lua->invoke(h, "tests/maps/hello/src/hello.lua", "greet",
90	                         args, sizes, 2,
91	                         buf, sizeof buf, &n);
92	    ASSERT(rc == 0);
93	    ASSERT(n == (int)strlen("Hi, World!"));
94	    buf[n] = '\0';
95	    ASSERT(strcmp(buf, "Hi, World!") == 0);
96	
97	    lua->teardown(h);
98	    spec_registry_destroy(r);
99	    return 1;
100	}
101	/* }}} */
