{
  "content":"/* tests/317-lua-bridge-test.c — Lua spec native↔JSON bridges.\n *\n * Loads langs/lua/spec.so through the spec registry and exercises\n * `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` together. The strategy is a\n * round-trip: feed a JSON string in via `json_to_native` (which\n * pushes a Lua value on the worker's lua_State), then yank it back\n * out via `native_to_json` (which reads top-of-stack and writes JSON\n * to a buffer). If the input and output match, both halves of the\n * bridge agree on the encoding.\n *\n * The round-trip approach keeps this test free of any direct\n * dependency on the LuaJIT headers — the bridges are the only thing\n * the test calls, and the lua_State is opaque from C's side. That\n * matches the cross-language wire-format design (issue 317): callers\n * never need to know Lua's internals to move data through it.\n *\n * Run from the project root so the spec dlopen path resolves.\n */\n\n#include \"011-spec-registry.h\"\n#include \"lang-spec.h\"\n\n#include <stdio.h>\n#include <stdlib.h>\n#include <string.h>\n#include <sys/stat.h>\n\n/* {{{ Test harness */\nstatic int g_pass = 0;\nstatic int g_fail = 0;\n\n#define ASSERT(cond) \\\n    do { if (!(cond)) { \\\n        fprintf(stderr, \"      %s:%d: %s\\n\", __FILE__, __LINE__, #cond); \\\n        return 0; \\\n    } } while (0)\n\n#define RUN(name) \\\n    do { \\\n        fprintf(stdout, \"  %-44s \", #name); fflush(stdout); \\\n        if (test_##name()) { fprintf(stdout, \"ok\\n\"); g_pass++; } \\\n        else                { fprintf(stdout, \"FAIL\\n\"); g_fail++; } \\\n    } while (0)\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ load_lua_spec() — boot the registry and find the lua spec */\nstatic const lang_spec_t *load_lua_spec(spec_registry_t **out_reg)\n{\n    char *err = NULL;\n    spec_registry_t *r = spec_registry_load(\"langs\", &err);\n    if (!r) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"registry load failed: %s\\n\", err ? err : \"(null)\");\n        free(err);\n        return NULL;\n    }\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = spec_registry_get(r, \"lua\");\n    if (!lua || !lua->init || !lua->teardown\n            || !lua->native_to_json || !lua->json_to_native) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"lua spec missing bridge callbacks\\n\");\n        spec_registry_destroy(r);\n        return NULL;\n    }\n    *out_reg = r;\n    return lua;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ roundtrip() — JSON → Lua stack → JSON, compare to expected */\n/* Convenience used by every test case. `expected` is the canonical\n * JSON the bridge should emit on the way back; if NULL, the input\n * itself is the expected output. Returns 1 on success. */\nstatic int roundtrip(const lang_spec_t *lua, void *h,\n                     const char *input, const char *expected)\n{\n    char out[512];\n    int  out_size = 0;\n    int  rc = lua->json_to_native(h, input, (int)strlen(input),\n                                  NULL, 0, NULL);\n    if (rc != 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"      json_to_native failed for '%s'\\n\", input);\n        return 0;\n    }\n    rc = lua->native_to_json(h, NULL, 0, out, (int)sizeof(out), &out_size);\n    if (rc != 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"      native_to_json failed for '%s'\\n\", input);\n        return 0;\n    }\n    out[out_size] = '\\0';\n    const char *want = expected ? expected : input;\n    if (strcmp(out, want) != 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"      mismatch: want '%s', got '%s'\\n\", want, out);\n        return 0;\n    }\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ test_scalars() — null, bool, integer, float, string */\nstatic int test_scalars(void)\n{\n    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);\n    ASSERT(lua);\n    void *h = lua->init(0);\n    ASSERT(h);\n\n    /* Integer-shaped Lua numbers round-trip as integers (3, not 3.0)\n     * because encode_value picks the int writer for whole values.\n     * \"null\" round-trips because nil → null → nil. */\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"null\",      NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"true\",      NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"false\",     NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"0\",         NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"42\",        NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"-17\",       NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"\\\"\\\"\",      NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"\\\"hello\\\"\", NULL));\n\n    lua->teardown(h);\n    spec_registry_destroy(r);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ test_arrays() — sequence tables round-trip as JSON arrays */\nstatic int test_arrays(void)\n{\n    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);\n    ASSERT(lua);\n    void *h = lua->init(0);\n\n    /* Empty array is the one shape we *can't* round-trip as []\n     * because an empty Lua table can't be told apart from {} on the\n     * way out (see encode_value's array-vs-object heuristic). The\n     * round-trip canonicalises [] to {} — a deliberate, documented\n     * choice. Every non-empty sequence works as expected. */\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[]\",                \"{}\"));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[1]\",               NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[1,2,3]\",           NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[\\\"a\\\",\\\"b\\\"]\",     NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[[1,2],[3,4]]\",     NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"[true,false,null]\", NULL));\n\n    lua->teardown(h);\n    spec_registry_destroy(r);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ test_objects() — keyed tables round-trip as JSON objects */\n/* Object key ordering inside a Lua table is not guaranteed to\n * match the input — Lua's table iteration order is implementation\n * defined. We test single-key objects (no ordering ambiguity) and\n * nested structure for the multi-key case. */\nstatic int test_objects(void)\n{\n    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);\n    ASSERT(lua);\n    void *h = lua->init(0);\n\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"{}\",            NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"{\\\"x\\\":1}\",     NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"{\\\"a\\\":\\\"b\\\"}\", NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"{\\\"nest\\\":{\\\"k\\\":42}}\", NULL));\n    ASSERT(roundtrip(lua, h, \"{\\\"items\\\":[1,2,3]}\",   NULL));\n\n    lua->teardown(h);\n    spec_registry_destroy(r);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ test_truncation() — undersized buffer is a loud error */\n/* Project convention forbids silent fallbacks. A too-small output\n * buffer must surface as a nonzero return from native_to_json,\n * not a truncated-but-success path. */\nstatic int test_truncation(void)\n{\n    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);\n    ASSERT(lua);\n    void *h = lua->init(0);\n\n    int rc = lua->json_to_native(h, \"[1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]\",\n                                 (int)strlen(\"[1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9]\"),\n                                 NULL, 0, NULL);\n    ASSERT(rc == 0);\n\n    char tiny[4];\n    int  out_size = 0;\n    rc = lua->native_to_json(h, NULL, 0, tiny, (int)sizeof(tiny), &out_size);\n    ASSERT(rc != 0);\n\n    lua->teardown(h);\n    spec_registry_destroy(r);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ test_parse_error() — malformed JSON returns nonzero */\nstatic int test_parse_error(void)\n{\n    spec_registry_t *r = NULL;\n    const lang_spec_t *lua = load_lua_spec(&r);\n    ASSERT(lua);\n    void *h = lua->init(0);\n\n    int rc = lua->json_to_native(h, \"{not json\", 9, NULL, 0, NULL);\n    ASSERT(rc != 0);\n\n    lua->teardown(h);\n    spec_registry_destroy(r);\n    return 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ main() */\nint main(void)\n{\n    struct stat st;\n    if (stat(\"langs/lua/spec.so\", &st) != 0) {\n        fprintf(stderr, \"317-lua-bridge-test: langs/lua/spec.so not built; \"\n                        \"run `make specs` first\\n\");\n        return 2;\n    }\n    printf(\"317-lua-bridge-test:\\n\");\n    RUN(scalars);\n    RUN(arrays);\n    RUN(objects);\n    RUN(truncation);\n    RUN(parse_error);\n    printf(\"\\n  %d passed, %d failed\\n\", g_pass, g_fail);\n    return g_fail == 0 ? 0 : 1;\n}\n/* }}} */\n",

### [225] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-20T02:29:55.232Z

Found a Lua/JSON impedance issue — `null` in an array becomes `nil`, and `lua_objlen` stops at the first hole, so `[true,false,null]` becomes a 2-element array. That's a real Lua semantics gotcha that deserves a documented test case rather than passing it off. Updating the test.

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 317 — Language spec JSON bridge for data boxes
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	A data box (`kind: data` in box JSON) emits a value the rest of
9	the graph routes downstream. Today the runner reads the box's
10	`ref` file as raw bytes and emits them — there's no concept of
11	"authoring this datum in a particular language using that
12	language's natural structures." If you want to declare a Lua
13	table as a data box's value, you either:
14	
15	- Hand-translate the table to JSON, save it as a `.json` file,
16	  and lose the Lua syntax niceties; or
17	- Make it a call box that returns the table, paying for a full
18	  spec invocation just to read a constant; or
19	- Hard-code the bytes inline in the box's `data` field, with no
20	  syntactic help from any host language.
21	
22	This is the wrong shape. **A box author writes idiomatic code in
23	the language of their choice.** That's the rule 307 establishes
24	for call boxes: a C-box author writes a plain C function, no
25	SoraMech-specific glue. The data-box equivalent should hold —
26	a Lua-data-box author should write a Lua file that returns
27	whatever they want returned (a table, a string, a number), and
28	SoraMech should know how to lift that into the graph as a JSON
29	wire value without the author writing a single SoraMech-specific
30	character.
31	
32	## Intended behavior
33	
34	Every language spec gains two callbacks, declared in
35	`lang_spec_t` (issue 303):
36	
37	```c
38	/* Serialize a native-language value into JSON text.
39	 *
40	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle from spec.init.
41	 *   src       — caller-meaningful pointer to the native value.
42	 *               Format is spec-specific (see "Native handle
43	 *               representation" below).
44	 *   src_size  — size hint, or 0 if the spec interprets `src`
45	 *               without a length.
46	 *   out_buf   — destination buffer for JSON text (caller-owned).
47	 *   out_cap   — capacity of out_buf.
48	 *   out_size  — set to the bytes written.
49	 *
50	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. Writes an error message
51	 * via the spec's existing error-reporting plumbing. */
52	int (*native_to_json)(void *ctx,
53	                      const void *src, uint32_t src_size,
54	                      char *out_buf, uint32_t out_cap, uint32_t *out_size);
55	
56	/* Inverse: parse JSON text into a native-language value. The
57	 * native value is stored wherever the spec considers natural
58	 * (Lua: pushed to the worker's lua_State stack; C: written into
59	 * a typed struct supplied by `dst`; Bash: written as a textual
60	 * representation back into dst).
61	 *
62	 *   ctx       — per-worker handle.
63	 *   src       — JSON text.
64	 *   src_size  — JSON text length in bytes.
65	 *   dst       — caller-supplied target; semantics per spec.
66	 *   dst_cap   — capacity, where meaningful.
67	 *   dst_size  — set to the bytes / native units consumed.
68	 *
69	 * Returns 0 on success, -1 on error. */
70	int (*json_to_native)(void *ctx,
71	                      const char *src, uint32_t src_size,
72	                      void *dst, uint32_t dst_cap, uint32_t *dst_size);
73	```
74	
75	These are the same fields 312's same-language fast path
76	reserves; this issue is the one that requires every spec to
77	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
78	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
79	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
80	
81	### The rule, restated
82	
83	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
84	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
85	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
86	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
87	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
88	
89	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
90	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
91	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
92	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
93	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
94	already produced JSON).
95	
96	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
97	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
98	other way around.**
99	
100	## Native handle representation
101	
102	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
103	spec documents what it accepts:
104	
105	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
106	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
107	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
108	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
109	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
110	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
111	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
112	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
113	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
114	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
115	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
116	  JSON shape.)
117	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
118	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
119	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
120	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
121	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
122	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
123	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
124	
125	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
126	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
127	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
128	whole point.
129	
130	## Data box semantics
131	
132	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
133	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
134	
135	```json
136	{
137	  "id": "config",
138	  "kind": "data",
139	  "lang": "lua",
140	  "ref": "config.lua"
141	}
142	```
143	
144	At graph load, the runner:
145	
146	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
147	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
148	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
149	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
150	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
151	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
152	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
153	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
154	   value to JSON text.
155	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
156	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
157	   from a language-native file.
158	
159	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
160	inline-data path applies unchanged.
161	
162	## Why every spec must implement these
163	
164	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
165	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
166	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
167	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
168	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
169	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
170	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
171	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
172	both so any language can play any role.
173	
174	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
175	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
176	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
177	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
178	loud rather than producing wrong output.
179	
180	## Suggested implementation steps
181	
182	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
183	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
184	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
185	   reserved.)
186	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
187	   spec:
188	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
189	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
190	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
191	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
192	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
193	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
194	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
195	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
196	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
197	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
198	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
199	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
200	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
201	4. Add a fixture: `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` with a Lua
202	   data box, a C consumer, and a Bash consumer all reading the
203	   same value. The integration check asserts each consumer
204	   sees the right data.
205	5. Update each spec's `*.info.md` to document the native handle
206	   shape its callbacks expect.
207	
208	## Relevant files
209	
210	- `langs/lang-spec.h` — extend the contract
211	- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` —
212	  implement the callbacks
213	- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.info.md` — document native handle
214	  semantics per spec
215	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — data-box dispatch path
216	- `tests/maps/cross-lang-data/` — new fixture (see step 4)
217	- `issues/307-c-language-spec.md` — same author-writes-natural-
218	  code principle; this issue extends it from call boxes to data
219	  boxes
220	- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — reuses the same
221	  spec callbacks for a different (performance) reason
222	
223	## Open questions
224	
225	- Bash's `json_to_native` is a near-identity because the
226	  scripts already deal in text. Is there value in having it
227	  validate and reformat for consistency? Probably yes; cheap
228	  insurance against malformed downstream input.
229	- Should the Lua spec auto-detect array-vs-object tables, or
230	  require the box author to annotate? Auto-detect (contiguous
231	  1-indexed integer keys → array; otherwise object) is what
232	  every other lua-to-json library does and is intuitive enough.
233	- C spec's `native_to_json` for arbitrary structs is a real
234	  rabbit hole. Tie scope to 307 — the same type declarations
235	  drive both the wrapper and the JSON walk. Until 307 lands,
236	  only scalar types are supported for C data boxes.
237	
```

### [257] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-20T02:34:55.924Z

Two parts to my reply. First the impedance question, then a survey of the in-progress files before I start committing.

## What the null-in-array case implies for the bridge

It's a load-bearing finding, not a curiosity. The wire format (JSON) carries strictly more shape than any one host language. Lua loses `null` inside sequences. C will lose JSON's tag-free number when it can't tell int from double without a type declaration. Bash will lose nested structure unless we mandate JSON-formatted output.

So the bridge is **not symmetric per-spec** — a value entering Lua-side may not come out structurally identical on the other side. This forks the design into two stances we need to pick between:

1. **Lossy round-trips are the box author's problem.** Same-language wires (issue 312) stay lossless; cross-language wires go through JSON and the user is told at wire-compile time when the source language can express something the destination can't. We add a compatibility check at graph-load (e.g. "Lua → C edge: target type `int[]` cannot contain null — your Lua data box returns an array with nil") and otherwise accept the limitation.

2. **The bridge invents a per-language convention to preserve JSON faithfully.** For Lua specifically: arrays carry an explicit `{n=3, [1]=true, [2]=false, [3]=nil}` shape; the bridge encodes/decodes around the `n` field. Heavier; intrudes on idiomatic Lua; but every value survives a round-trip.

My instinct is **(1)** — it matches the project's "bend to the language, not the other way around" rule. The user wrote a Lua data box; they wrote idiomatic Lua; if their value can't be expressed in the destination language that's a real graph-compile error, not something we hide behind a sentinel. Option (2) only buys you fidelity for cross-language wires where both sides happen to be lossy in different ways.

But this is a directional call — want me to record it in issue 317 as the decided stance, or hold off until you've thought it through?

## Surveying the in-progress files before committing

Let me first read the modified issue files and untracked new files so I know which changes belong to which ticket.

 ## Lifetime
 
-Slots are owned by the box that holds the input port. They live
-from graph load until run end. There is no per-slot reference

+A slot — the named input port on a box — is owned by its box and
+lives from graph load until run end. That hasn't changed. What
+has changed is the values that pass through slots: they are
+reference-counted memory chunks served by a single unified
+allocator (see "Allocation strategy" below), not malloc'd memory
+that lives the whole run.
+
+Every value handed to a consumer carries a reference. When a
+consumer is done with a value, it drops its reference. When the
+last reference drops, the chunk holding the value goes back to
+the allocator's free-lists, available for the next request. Two
+neighbor-merge steps and an on-demand sweep keep fragmentation
+under control (described in the allocator section).
+
+The earlier note in this document — "no per-slot reference
+counting" — referred to the box's own slot record, not to the
+values flowing through it. The slot record itself is still
+allocated once at graph load and not freed until run end. The
+values inside the slot are the things that get refcounted, freed,
+and recycled. The distinction matters: there's one slot per
+input port, and many values flow through that slot over the life
+of a run.
+
+Run termination is still governed by the pool's active-task
+counter (issue 301): when no task is running and no further
+spawns are pending, the run is over. The allocator and refcount
+machinery don't influence termination — they just ensure memory
+gets returned to the free-lists as values become unreferenced
+during the run.
 
 ### No wait lists
 
@@ -305,128 +325,157 @@ synchronization primitive.
 The pool's queue is the only queue. There is no separate "blocked
 tasks" structure.
 
-## Allocation strategy: size-class free lists with coalescing fallback
-
-The slot store uses a segregated free-list allocator. The map is
-analyzed at compile time to enumerate every distinct slot size the
-graph will need (each call box declares its output slot size; each
-queued-input ring declares its total size from cell capacity × queue
-depth). The allocator builds one free list per distinct size class.
-
-Variable-size and unknown-size payloads (a long LLM response, an
-array whose length is not known until runtime) are handled by a
-separate **large-value heap** described below — not by allocating
-huge slots up front.
-
-### The lists
-Each size class is an array (or linked list) of free slots of exactly
-that size. At startup, each list is pre-populated with enough slots
-to cover the static box count for that size. Lists grow during the
-run if demand exceeds the pre-allocation.
-
-Each list has its own mutex — allocation and deallocation in different
-size classes never contend with each other.
-
-### Allocation
-Given a request for size N bytes:
-1. Find the smallest size class with `class_size ≥ N`.
-2. If that class has a free slot, pop it. Done.
-3. Otherwise, try the next larger size class. Repeat.
-4. If a larger class hits, the slot is oversized for the request.
-   Try to slice the unused tail into a residual slot. If the residual
-   is at least as large as the smallest size class, push it onto that
-   class's list. Otherwise skip the slice — oversize the allocation
-   and live with the waste until the slot is freed.
-5. If no size class has a free slot, attempt to coalesce: walk the
-   address-ordered chunk list looking for adjacent free chunks that
-   together meet the requested size. If found, merge them and return
-   the combined chunk.
-6. If coalescing fails, grow the region (see "Growth" below) and
-   append the new slots to the appropriate lists. Then try again.
-7. After steps 5 or 6 complete, queue a deferred cleanup task on the
-   pool. Cleanup runs on a worker thread later and walks the
-   chunk list to opportunistically coalesce contiguous free chunks
-   into the largest possible chunks. Cleanup is best-effort — it
-   does not block any other thread. If memory fragments faster than
-   cleanup can keep up, the next allocation grows the heap; that's
-   fine.
-
-### Deallocation

381
+fragments crosses a threshold — but rejected for now. It works, but
+it needs the threshold tuned, and the two triggers above cover the
+same ground without that tuning step.
+### Physical-order chunk list (for the merge steps)
+Both the eager neighbor-merge and the deeper sweep need to know
+which chunks are physically adjacent in memory. The size-class
+free-lists don't carry that information — they're indexed by size,
+not address.
+The supporting structure is a doubly-linked list of all chunks (free
+and allocated) in address order. Splits and merges are O(1) given a
+chunk pointer. Neighbor lookup is O(1). The cost is pointer-chasing
+during the deeper sweep, but the sweep only runs at the two triggers
+above, not on the hot path.
+If profiling later shows traversal cost hurting at sweep time, the
+list can be swapped for a sorted-by-address array of `(start, end)`
+pairs with an auxiliary index — the allocator's external API doesn't
+change.
+### Why one allocator instead of two
+The split design existed because the original two-tier scheme had a
+"large-value heap" carved out for variable-size payloads, on the
+theory that mixing variable and fixed sizes in the same pool would
+complicate the fixed-size path. That reasoning doesn't survive
+contact with reference counting and free-list recycling: with
+refcount-driven free, the fixed-size path stays fast because most
+requests match a declared class and pop in one step regardless of
+what else is in the region.
+Splitting them also meant the variable-size pool had to grow
+forever, because writing the same allocator twice — once with
+reclamation, once without — was more work than was budgeted in early
+phase 3. That's the position the current code is in. Unified gets us
+the reclamation everywhere, with no extra design work.
+### Growth (last resort)
+When both doors fail and the deep sweep can't produce a fitting
+chunk, the allocator grows by asking the operating system for a new
+region, dividing it into chunks per the declared size classes (or
+into a single large chunk if the request was for an unknown size
+beyond all declared classes), and threading the new chunks into the
+free-lists and the address-ordered list.
+Growth is the rare case. With pre-warmed free-lists sized to static
+demand and refcount-driven recycling, a well-behaved graph never
+reaches growth — every allocation either pops a pre-warmed chunk or
+recycles one whose last reference just dropped.
@@ -585,3 +634,51 @@ What's still deferred within 302:
+### Retraction of the monotonic-growth deferral — 2026-05-19
+The two deferrals listed immediately above this entry no longer
+reflect the design. Monotonic growth was being described as
+"acceptable for current fixtures." It isn't acceptable — it's a
+memory leak by design, and any long-running workload (multi-day
+runs, anything driven by an LLM in a loop, anything that
+accumulates) will reproduce it as a real problem. Calling it
+deferred made it easier to skip; calling it a leak makes it clear
+why the skip isn't safe.
+The design above this implementation log has been rewritten
+accordingly. There are no longer two stores. There is one
+allocator that serves both compile-time-known sizes and
+runtime-only-known sizes from a shared pool, with reference
+counting driving deallocation, an O(1) eager neighbor-merge on
+return, and a deeper sweep on the two triggers described in the
+allocator section ("at the moment an allocation would otherwise
+fail" and "at quiescence between task batches"). Both triggers
+are deterministic — they react to the program's own behavior
+rather than to wall-clock time.
+What this means in terms of work remaining:

 # }}}
 
+# {{{ parser_tests() — issue 232: unit tests for langs/<lang>/parser.js
+# Run Node's built-in test runner over each per-language parser test
+# file and fold the result into the surrounding pass/fail counters.
+# Each .mjs file is one logical "test" from this script's perspective;
+# Node prints its own subtest breakdown on failure for the diagnostic.
+parser_tests() {
+    if ! command -v node >/dev/null 2>&1; then
+        printf "  %-44s SKIP — node not installed\n" "parser tests (lua, bash)"
+        return
+    fi
+    for tf in "$DIR/tests/232-lua-parser-test.mjs" \
+              "$DIR/tests/233-bash-parser-test.mjs" \
+              "$DIR/tests/234-language-spec-js-test.mjs"; do
+        local label
+        label=$(basename "$tf")
+        printf "  %-44s " "$label"
+        local out
+        out=$(SORAMECH_DIR="$DIR" node --test "$tf" 2>&1)
+        local rc=$?
+        if [[ $rc -eq 0 ]]; then
+            printf "ok\n"
+            pass=$((pass + 1))
+        else
+            printf "FAIL\n"
+            echo "$out" | sed 's/^/      /'
+            fail=$((fail + 1))
+            failures+=("$label: node --test exit=$rc")
+        fi
+    done
+}
+parser_tests
+# }}}
+
 echo
 echo "  $pass passed, $fail failed"
```

# 217 — Variadic ports and the language-spec variadic shape

## Status
open — Part A and Part B mechanics are in the codebase; Part C is
the active design question

## What this issue is now

The original issue was titled "Concat box with per-input variadic
ports and auto-grow/shrink." That framing has aged out:

- There is no dedicated **concat box kind**. `M.concat(sep, ...)`
  lives in `libs/text.lua` and is invoked by any call box that
  imports it. The variadic UI work was never specific to concat.
- The original "split returns a JSON-encoded array on the single
  output wire" story was a phase-2 transport assumption. Phase 3
  (issue 312, same-language wire fast path) classifies each edge
  and only JSON-encodes when crossing a language boundary. So
  `M.split` returning a Lua array is correct in-language and the
  runtime bridges if the downstream box is a different language.

The issue is now about the **editor's variadic-port mechanic** and
how it interacts with the **language spec** — specifically, what
the editor's `var` toggle means and what governs whether it's
offered.

## Part A — `libs/text.lua` *(shipped)*

`libs/text.lua` ships `M.concat(sep, ...)`, `M.split(text, sep)`,
and (via issue 237) `upper`, `lower`, `trim`, `replace`,
`replace_first`, `contains`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `length`,
`substring`. All are pure string operations consumed by call
boxes; no special box kind exists.

`M.split` is most useful paired with the iterator box (issue 221)
so each part fans out to its own dispatch. Without the iterator
it's just "an array on a wire" — legitimate but flat.

## Part B — Editor variadic data model *(shipped)*

# 232 — Test coverage map

## Status
ongoing (test inventory; updated each time tests are added or
removed)

## Why this exists

This issue is the project-wide answer to "what's tested, what
isn't, and what runs the tests." It folds together two earlier
threads:

- **316** (now folded in here) — a cheap, build-free unit-test
  runner script. Its scope lives under "Test runners → Planned"
  below.
- The parser-test work that opened this issue (Node-side tests
  for `langs/<name>/parser.js`).

A related but separate issue is **311** — phase-3 integration
tests and the JSONL run-output format. 311's scope is the JSONL
writer + the `tests/maps/<name>/` fixtures driven through
`soramech-pool`; the coverage of those fixtures shows up in the
"End-to-end fixtures" section below, but the writer/format work
stays in 311.

Without a coverage view, gaps accumulate silently: tests get
written for the area someone happens to be touching, and the rest
drifts uncovered. The checklist below is that view, updated every
time tests are added or removed.

Every time a new test file lands or a test gap is closed, the
checklist below is updated. When a new module is added, a row goes
into the appropriate section showing it has no coverage yet.

## How to read the table

- `[x]` — has a dedicated test file (path in the row).
- `[ ]` — no dedicated test; may be exercised indirectly through
  integration fixtures, but not directly verified.
- `[~]` — partially covered; only some surface area is tested.

==> /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/239-literal-port-shrinks-to-nodule-with-name-on-left.md <==
# 239 — Literal-bound input ports shrink to a nodule, port name moves to the left

## Status
open

## Current behavior

Per issue 235 (complete), an input port whose literal value is
set renders with the value in place of the port name. The dot
on the box's left edge stays full-size, and the port name
disappears from the canvas — only the value is visible. The
inspector still owns the name, and the dot still works as a
wire-grab target even though wiring into a port with a literal
value would severs that literal on connect.

What the user reading the map can see at a glance:

```
●  "hello world"
```

The port's variable name (`text`, `prompt`, whatever it is in
the underlying function signature) is invisible on the canvas.
A reader has to open the inspector to recover the binding.

## Intended behavior

A port with a literal value rendered in three pieces, in three
positions, in this order left-to-right:

```
text   ·   "hello world"
```

- **Port name** (`text`) — moved to the LEFT of the box's edge,
  in the gutter just outside the box body. This is the
  variable's identity, and 235's value-takes-over rendering
  hid it. Pulling it out to the left puts the name back in
  plain sight WITHOUT giving up the value display.
- **Nodule** — a smaller, denser dot sitting where the regular
```

### [309] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-20T02:43:12.284Z

Got it. Three things first: revise the fidelity stance in 317, investigate the C parser gap, then start committing.

## Why fidelity might be lost — and what to do about it

You're right to push back. Re-deriving from your stance:

- **Lua → JSON**: a Lua table `{[1]=true, [2]=false, [3]=nil}` has only TWO entries in Lua's actual storage — nil isn't stored, so the encoder physically can't see that slot 3 was "meant to be there." Lua's tables can't represent "explicit null in position 3" idiomatically. To preserve fidelity we have to intrude — e.g. an `n` field that records the intended length, or a sidecar null-sentinel, or a JSON-side type tag.
- **JSON → Lua → JSON round-trip**: the loss happens at the JSON → Lua step (nil-in-array vanishes). So the decoder must leave behind a marker so the encoder can reconstruct.

Your prescription, applied: introduce a Lua-side convention (`n` field or similar) at decode time, read it at encode time, emit the full array with embedded nulls. Idiomatic Lua tables (no `n`, no holes) still encode as arrays normally. The intrusion only surfaces when JSON shape requires it.

Let me record this directly in 317 so the next implementation round has a clear directive.

3	// The C side of the spec (spec.c) tells the pool runner how to
4	// load and invoke Lua functions. This file tells the *editor* the
5	// language-level facts the UI needs — things that are true of
6	// every function in the language, not facts about any particular
7	// function's source.
8	//
9	// `variadic_shape` answers: how does this language accept "extra"
10	// positional arguments at call time? "positional" means every
11	// function can be called with N args, period — the language's
12	// calling convention drops or exposes extras as it sees fit. For
13	// Lua that's literally always: `function f(a)` called with five
14	// args silently keeps the first and `...` would have captured the
15	// rest. The fact that the *source* doesn't write `...` is the
16	// function author's choice, not a runtime constraint.
17	//
18	// The editor reads this constant to decide whether the inspector's
19	// `var` toggle is offered for ports on a Lua box. The toggle is a
20	// data-model affordance for grouping N inputs onto one logical
21	// port — not a guarantee that the function will use them. Mis-wire
22	// is the user's call; the editor's job is to show what the source
23	// declares (via parser.js) so the user can see the arity they're

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 231 — Move per-language signature parsers out of the file browser into `langs/<name>/parser.js`
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	
8	`assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` carries language-specific code:
9	- `parse_lua(content)` — extracts `function M.<name>(<params>)`
10	  declarations and scans for `return` statements to enumerate
11	  outputs.
12	- `parse_bash(content)` — extracts `<name>() {` declarations and
13	  uses `parse_bash_inputs` to count `$N` references.
14	- `parse_functions(content, filename)` — dispatches on file
15	  extension to one of the above.
16	
17	C has no parser yet. Languages a user might add (Python, Rust, …)
18	have no path to a parser.
19	
20	The lexer infrastructure (issue 223, complete) already establishes
21	the right pattern: per-language files under `langs/<name>/`,
22	loaded on demand by the editor through dynamic `import()`. The
23	signature parsers belong there, not in the editor.
24	
25	## Intended behavior
26	
27	Each language directory carries a `parser.js` ES module exporting
28	a single function:
29	
30	```js
31	// langs/<name>/parser.js
32	export function parse_functions(content) {
33	  return [
34	    {
35	      name: 'write_result',
36	      inputs: ['text'],
37	      outputs: [],            // empty array → sink box (issue 226)
38	      variadic_base: null,    // or 'args' for f(a, ...)
39	    },
40	    // ...
41	  ];
42	}
43	```
44	
45	The editor's file browser drops its `parse_lua` / `parse_bash` /
46	`parse_functions` and asks the per-language parser instead. Same
47	lazy-load pattern as the lexers: a registry keyed by file
48	extension, dynamic `import()` on first need, cached per session.
49	Files with an unknown extension fall back to "no parser available"
50	— the file browser displays the file name but offers no
51	function-list step.
52	
53	### Extended return shape
54	
55	The parser returns a richer record than the current shape:
56	- `name` — function name (existing).
57	- `inputs` — array of parameter names in declaration order
58	  (existing).
59	- `outputs` — array of return-value names; empty for sinks
60	  (existing).
61	- `variadic_base` — string or null. Set when the function
62	  declaration has a variadic tail (Lua `...`, Bash `"$@"`, C
63	  `va_list`). Editor uses it to auto-flip the var toggle for that
64	  port. Existing parsers don't compute this; new field, optional
65	  for parsers that don't bother.
66	
67	### Why this matters
68	
69	The file browser is the editor's single entry point for "import
70	function from source." Any language-specific logic in it is the
71	wrong level of abstraction. Moving it puts the editor on equal
72	footing with the runtime: both treat languages as pluggable, both
73	discover capabilities through `langs/<name>/`, neither has a
74	hardcoded list.
75	
76	It also makes adding a new language a self-contained job: drop
77	`langs/<name>/parser.js`, `langs/<name>/lexer.js`, and (phase 3)
78	`langs/<name>/spec.so` into `langs/`, and the editor and the
79	runner both pick it up.
80	
81	## Suggested implementation sequence
82	
83	1. Create `langs/lua/parser.js` with the existing `parse_lua` /
84	   `parse_lua_returns` logic, exporting `parse_functions`.
85	2. Create `langs/bash/parser.js` with the existing `parse_bash` /
86	   `parse_bash_inputs` logic.
87	3. Optionally create `langs/c/parser.js` — a minimal one that
88	   extracts `int name(int a, char *b)` style declarations from
89	   header files. Defer if not needed yet.
90	4. Add a parser registry to `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` mirroring
91	   the lexer registry in `008-source-view.js`:
92	   ```js
93	   const EXT_TO_LANG = { lua: 'lua', sh: 'bash', bash: 'bash' };
94	   const parser_cache = {};
95	   async function load_parser(lang) { /* dynamic import */ }
96	   ```
97	5. Replace `parse_functions(content, filename)` body with a lookup
98	   into the registry. The function becomes async; existing callers
99	   already `await` async file fetches, so this slots in.
100	6. Delete `parse_lua`, `parse_bash`, `parse_lua_returns`,
101	   `parse_bash_inputs`, `lua_fn_end` from `007-filebrowser.js`.
102	7. (Optional, separate change) Wire `variadic_base` into the
103	   on-select handler so the editor auto-flips the var toggle.
104	
105	## Open questions
106	
107	- **Test coverage**: parsers are easy to break with a regex tweak.
108	  A small set of fixture sources (`maps/_test/parser-fixtures/*.lua`,
109	  `*.sh`) and a JS test harness would catch regressions. Out of
110	  scope for this issue, worth a follow-up.
111	- **Parsing failure shape**: a parser that throws should be
112	  reported as "parser error" not "no parser." Add a try/catch in
113	  the registry layer with a clear message.
114	
115	## Relevant files
116	
117	- `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js` — current parsers move out
118	- `langs/lua/parser.js` — new
119	- `langs/bash/parser.js` — new
120	- `langs/lua/lexer.js`, `langs/bash/lexer.js` — peer modules; same
121	  loading pattern
122	- `assets/js/008-source-view.js` — reference implementation for
123	  the lazy lexer registry
124	- `issues/completed/223-syntax-highlighting-via-user-lexers.md` —
125	  established the `langs/<name>/<file>` server-serve route and the
126	  dynamic-import loading model
127	- `docs/005-language-specs.md` — add a "parser interface" section
128	  alongside the existing "lexer interface"
129	
```

{
  "command":"git commit -m \"$(cat <<'EOF'\n016 — one allocator to serve every slot-store value\n\nA reference-counted, size-class-segregated allocator that hands out\nchunks of memory to whichever subsystem asks. The slot store, the\nlarge-value heap, anything else that holds wire values — they all\nend up funneled through the same pool. Eager neighbor-merge on\nreturn keeps fragmentation honest for the common case; a deeper\nsweep runs on demand when an allocation would otherwise have to\ngrow the region. Pre-warmed free-lists handle the static class\nsizes a map declares at compile time; growth is the rare last\nresort. Thread-safe via per-class mutexes — different sizes never\ncontend.\n\nThis is the standalone module. Wiring it into the slot store is the\nnext slice of issue 302.\n\nCo-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context) <noreply@anthropic.com>\nEOF\n)\"",

{
  "new_string":"    case LUA_TTABLE: {\n        /* n-field convention (issue 317, fidelity directive):\n         *\n         * If the table carries `n` as a non-negative integer, that\n         * field declares \"this is a JSON array of length n; every\n         * missing integer key in 1..n is an explicit null on the\n         * wire.\" That's how the decoder records arrays it pushed\n         * onto the stack — a full round-trip of [true,false,null]\n         * leaves `{n=3, [1]=true, [2]=false}` here, and we have to\n         * emit `[true,false,null]` from it. Idiomatic Lua tables\n         * (no n field) still take the existing heuristic path.\n         *\n         * The intrusion of an explicit `n` field on values pushed\n         * by the decoder is the price of preserving JSON fidelity\n         * through Lua's nil-as-absence storage model. Documented\n         * in issue 317. */\n        lua_getfield(L, abs_idx, \"n\");\n        int  has_declared_len = 0;\n        long declared_len     = 0;\n        if (lua_type(L, -1) == LUA_TNUMBER) {\n            lua_Number nn = lua_tonumber(L, -1);\n            long       ni = (long)nn;\n            if ((lua_Number)ni == nn && ni >= 0) {\n                has_declared_len = 1;\n                declared_len     = ni;\n            }\n        }\n        lua_pop(L, 1);\n\n        if (has_declared_len) {\n            json_writer_array(w);\n            for (long i = 1; i <= declared_len; i++) {\n                lua_rawgeti(L, abs_idx, (int)i);\n                int top = lua_gettop(L);\n                /* Missing keys land here as nil and encode_value\n                 * emits json null for them — exactly the round-trip\n                 * behavior the n-field convention promises. */\n                int r = encode_value(L, top, w);\n                lua_pop(L, 1);\n                if (r != 0) return -1;\n            }\n            json_writer_end(w);\n            return 0;\n        }\n\n        /* No n field — fall back to the existing heuristic: every\n         * key in 1..N and no others → array; anything else → object.\n         * Empty tables go through the object path because JSON `[]`\n         * vs `{}` is a distinction Lua can't natively express, and\n         * the n-field convention is the way to ask for `[]`. */\n        int n = (int)lua_objlen(L, abs_idx);\n        int total = 0;\n        lua_pushnil(L);\n        while (lua_next(L, abs_idx) != 0) {\n            total++;\n            lua_pop(L, 1);\n        }\n        int is_array = (n > 0 && total == n);\n\n        if (is_array) {\n            json_writer_array(w);\n            for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {\n                lua_rawgeti(L, abs_idx, i);\n                int top = lua_gettop(L);\n                int r = encode_value(L, top, w);\n                lua_pop(L, 1);\n                if (r != 0) return -1;\n            }\n            json_writer_end(w);\n        } else {",

  "old_string":"    case LUA_TTABLE: {\n        /* Array vs object: we treat a table as an array when its\n         * # length is positive AND a full key walk turns up exactly\n         * that many entries (no holes, no extra string keys). Empty\n         * tables encode as {} — a deliberate choice; JSON's [] vs {}\n         * distinction has no Lua equivalent and the object form\n         * keeps round-trips honest when downstream code expects a\n         * map. Future: an explicit array marker (issue 317 follow-up)\n         * if this becomes a footgun. */\n        int n = (int)lua_objlen(L, abs_idx);\n        int total = 0;\n        lua_pushnil(L);\n        while (lua_next(L, abs_idx) != 0) {\n            total++;\n            lua_pop(L, 1);\n        }\n        int is_array = (n > 0 && total == n);\n\n        if (is_array) {\n            json_writer_array(w);\n            for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {\n                lua_rawgeti(L, abs_idx, i);\n                int top = lua_gettop(L);\n                int r = encode_value(L, top, w);\n                lua_pop(L, 1);\n                if (r != 0) return -1;\n            }\n            json_writer_end(w);\n        } else {",

  "new_string":"    /* The n-field convention (issue 317, stage 2) preserves every\n     * array shape JSON can express — including the empty array,\n     * trailing nulls, embedded nulls, and leading nulls. {
  "new_string":"### 2026-05-19 — stage 2: Lua array fidelity\n\nStage 1 lost `null` inside arrays — `[true,false,null]` round-tripped\nas `[true,false]`. Stage 2 fixes it by applying the n-field\nconvention from the fidelity directive below.\n\nWhat changed in the code:\n\n- `decode_node` (JSON → Lua): the JSON_ARRAY case now creates a Lua\n  table with a string key `n` set to the array's length, then\n  fills integer keys 1..n. JSON `null` elements use `lua_rawseti`\n  with nil — which deletes the key, leaving an absent slot. The\n  table now carries enough information to reconstruct the array\n  losslessly.\n\n- `encode_value` (Lua → JSON): table emission reads `n` first. If\n  the field is a non-negative integer, the table is treated as a\n  declared-length array — the encoder walks 1..n and emits null\n  for any missing slot via the existing nil → null path. Tables\n  without `n` fall through to the existing array-vs-object\n  heuristic for idiomatic Lua values.\n\nWhat the tests prove now:\n\n- `[]`, `[null]`, `[null,null]`, `[null,1,2]`, `[1,null,3]`,\n  `[1,2,null]`, `[true,false,null]` all round-trip exactly.\n\nWhat this costs:\n\n- Lua tables that have crossed the bridge carry a `n` field\n  visible to `pairs()`. The fidelity directive accepts this:\n  \"We can intrude on idiomatic Lua because we're fundamentally\n  translating from Lua to JSON.\" Box authors who write Lua\n  tables by hand and want them treated as JSON arrays can also\n  set `n` themselves; otherwise the existing heuristic still\n  catches `{1, 2, 3}`-shaped tables.",
  "replace_all":false,
  "old_string":"**Known impedance-mismatch case (documented in the test):** `null`\ninside a JSON array becomes Lua `nil`, and `lua_objlen` truncates\nthe sequence at the first hole. The bridge round-trips\n`[true,false,null]` as `[true,false]`. This is fundamental Lua\nsemantics and is asserted explicitly rather than glossed over.\n\nThis stage-1 lossiness is **not the final design.** See the\nfidelity directive below — stage 2 must preserve the JSON shape\nthrough Lua via an explicit length field, accepting that this\nintrudes on idiomatic Lua.",
  "file_path":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/issues/317-spec-json-bridge-for-data-boxes.md"
}

- **Decoder** sets `t.n = len` and uses `lua_rawseti` with nil to leave deletion-of-key (no key) for null elements.
- **Tests**: 5/5, including the new `[null]`, `[null,1,2]`, `[1,null,3]`, `[null,null]`, `[true,false,null]` cases that stage 1 truncated.
- **Trade documented**: a Lua table that came through the bridge carries a visible `n` key — the fidelity directive accepted this.

Full suite green. Commit `82690ad`.

Working tree is back to just the held `229` doc. Where next? Options on the table:

1. **317 next slice — type-tag wrapper for ambiguity** (e.g. int vs float when Lua's whole-number `2` should reach a C `float` slot as `2.0`). Pairs with the C bridge work below.
2. **Bash bridge** — near-identity, smallest of the three, gets us one step closer to data boxes working end-to-end. No `handle` needed since bash scripts already deal in text.
3. **C bridge** — depends on 307's typed wrapper. Scalar-only first cut still useful.
4. **Wire the bridges into dispatch** (issue 312's classification path) so cross-language edges actually call them. Without this step the bridges are dead code on the wire.
5. **229** — now that 317 stage 2 is in, your "read/write box-kinds" design has a working JSON bridge to assume.

I'd suggest **4** — bridges are useless until dispatch calls them, and even the Lua-Lua case benefits from the round-trip going through them in the cross-language-edge case. What's your call?

### [500] type=user
ts: 2026-05-20T03:08:07.233Z

_tool_result:_
```
1	# 229 — `read` and `write` box kinds (language-aware file IO)
2	
3	## Status
4	open (redesigned 2026-05-19 — supersedes the earlier "data source +
5	file_write sink" and the still-earlier "data kind / literal-or-file
6	unified source" proposals, both kept below for history)
7	
8	## Current behavior
9	
10	The `data` kind is recognized by the schema and accepted by the
11	loader, but has no runtime semantics distinct from `call`. It
12	carries `ref` and `fn` like a call box and runs through whichever
13	language driver matches the file extension. The green badge on
14	the canvas is purely cosmetic — the box behaves exactly like
15	`call`.
16	
17	`libs/files.lua::read_text` and friends cover the actual "read a
18	text file" case (issue 216, complete) via regular call boxes,
19	but only from Lua. A Bash function that needs file contents has
20	to wire through a Lua read box, which is a language-coupling no
21	graph reader expects.
22	
23	## Intended behavior — two kinds, `read` and `write`
24	
25	Both are dispatch-layer primitives — no language driver runs for
26	their core IO. The language specs DO participate at the wire
27	boundary so the value crosses cross-language seams natively (see
28	"language-aware IO" below). The phase-3 entry point lives in
29	`src/012-dispatch.c`; the phase-2 mirror lives in
30	`src/004-executor.lua` so the editor's "test run" works the same.
31	
32	### `read` — emits a value
33	
34	A `read` box has one input port and one output port. The output
35	is **always trusted to produce a value** — that's the box's
36	single job, and downstream consumers can rely on it.
37	
38	| port | direction | type   | meaning                            |
39	|------|-----------|--------|-------------------------------------|
40	| path | in        | string | path to the file on disk            |
41	| (single output) | out | any | the value to emit                 |
42	
43	No `ref`, no `fn`. The box decides what to emit using this
44	precedence:
45	
46	1. If the inspector carries an **inline literal value** for the
47	   box, emit that directly. **The `path` input port hides on the
48	   canvas** in this mode — there's no file involved, and a path
49	   port on a box that doesn't read a file would just confuse the
50	   reader.
51	2. Else if `path` is supplied (via wire or via an inline literal
52	   path on the input port), read that file from disk and emit its
53	   contents.
54	3. Else error — a `read` with neither a literal nor a path is a
55	   misconfiguration the validator should catch.
56	
57	### `write` — emits a done signal
58	
59	| port  | direction | type   | meaning                            |
60	|-------|-----------|--------|-------------------------------------|
61	| path  | in        | string | destination path                    |
62	| value | in        | any    | the content to write                |
63	| (single output) | out | boolean | `true` when the write completed |
64	
65	The output is intentionally minimal — a boolean done signal
66	that downstream graphs can fan into "and now the file is on
67	disk, fire the next thing" patterns. It's also easy to ignore
68	when no one cares: per the unwired-output discard rule
69	(documented when this issue lands), a `write` with no wire on
70	its output produces the boolean and drops it.
71	
72	Atomic write: write to `path.tmp`, fsync, rename to `path`.
73	Same convention `soramech-data` already uses.
74	
75	### Canvas colors
76	
77	- `read` keeps the existing green for data boxes (`#4caf7d`) so
78	  source-of-truth-for-a-value boxes read consistently from old
79	  maps that still call them `data`.
80	- `write` gets **orange cheddar — `#d4762a`** (vivid, dark side,
81	  warm). The contrast against `read`'s green makes source vs
82	  sink visually obvious at a glance.
83	
84	### Why two kinds and not one unified `data` box
85	
86	A previous revision of this issue proposed a single `data` kind
87	that could be source, sink, or constant depending on its
88	configuration. That collapsed to ambiguity:
89	
90	- Two ways to source the output (literal vs file) needed a
91	  precedence rule.
92	- Read and write both want `path` — a "wrote without meaning to"
93	  footgun lurks when the user forgets to wire `value`.
94	- The output port carries fundamentally different semantics by
95	  mode (file contents vs done signal vs literal).
96	- A canvas reader can't tell what the box does from its shape.
97	
98	Source vs sink IS the natural seam. `read` always emits a value
99	(literal or file). `write` always commits a value to disk.
100	Different action, different identity, different color.
101	
102	## Language-aware IO (depends on issue 317)
103	
104	Every cross-language seam in the graph goes through the
105	`native_to_json` / `json_to_native` callbacks issue 317 adds to
106	each `lang_spec_t`. `read` and `write` use that same machinery
107	so a Lua data file feeding a C consumer arrives as a native C
108	value, and a C struct flowing into a `write` is serialized via
109	the C spec rather than blindly stringified by the dispatch
110	layer.
111	
112	### `read` per-downstream-wire fan-out
113	
114	A `read` box's single output port can have **N downstream
115	wires**, each ending at a box in possibly different languages.
116	The dispatch layer:
117	
118	1. Sources the value (literal or file bytes).
119	2. Iterates the box's outgoing wires. For each:
120	   a. Looks up the target box's language via its `ref` extension
121	      (or the routing layer's pre-classified `lang` from issue 305).
122	   b. Calls that language's `json_to_native` to lift the value
123	      into the target language's native form.
124	   c. Hands the native value to the target box's input slot.
125	
126	If three wires go to a C box, a Lua box, and a Bash box, the
127	dispatch loop runs the conversion three times — once per spec.
128	**Downstream boxes treat the `read` box's output as native to
129	their own language.** No SoraMech-specific glue at the consumer.
130	
131	For files on disk, `read` treats the file contents as JSON text —
132	the canonical wire format — and feeds those bytes to each
133	downstream spec's `json_to_native`. A Lua user writing a file by
134	hand still writes JSON (or uses a `write` box upstream, which
135	produces JSON anyway).
136	
137	### `write` per-upstream-wire dispatch
138	
139	A `write` box's `value` input is fed by exactly one upstream
140	wire. The dispatch layer:
141	
142	1. Looks up the source box's language.
143	2. Calls that language's `native_to_json` to canonicalize the
144	   value to JSON text.
145	3. Writes the JSON bytes to `path` atomically.
146	
147	If the source is a Lua box returning a table, the Lua spec
148	encodes the table to JSON. If the source is a C box returning a
149	struct, the C spec walks the type. The user never sees the
150	serialization step — it's the spec's job, not the box author's.
151	
152	### Why this matters
153	
154	The user's mental model is "I write code in my language, the
155	data crosses the wire, the other side gets it in their
156	language." `read` and `write` should reinforce that, not break
157	it. Either box would otherwise be the place where the user is
158	forced to think about JSON shapes, byte order, or string
159	escapes. With the spec-driven IO callbacks, all of that lives
160	where it belongs — in the language specs.
161	
162	### Dependency relationship with 317
163	
164	229 cannot land cleanly without 317. The cross-language emit/write
165	behavior described above IS 317's contract; this issue just names
166	the runtime primitives that make first use of it. Implementation
167	order: 317 → 229. If 229 lands first with a stub "everything goes
168	through bash-style text identity," the result is plain string IO
169	with no Lua-table / C-struct support, which is a regression from
170	the design goal.
171	
172	## Schema
173	
174	**read** (default file-source form):
175	```json
176	{
177	  "id": "load_cfg",
178	  "kind": "read",
179	  "inputs": [{ "name": "path" }],
180	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
181	}
182	```
183	
184	**read** with inline literal value (no path port):
185	```json
186	{
187	  "id": "default_prompt",
188	  "kind": "read",
189	  "value": "You are a helpful assistant.",
190	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
191	}
192	```
193	
194	**read** with inline literal path (path port still present
195	on canvas if no `value` is set, but the port's label shows
196	the literal — same convention as issue 235):
197	```json
198	{
199	  "id": "load_cfg",
200	  "kind": "read",
201	  "inputs": [{ "name": "path", "value": "config.json" }],
202	  "ui": { "x": 100, "y": 100 }
203	}
204	```
205	
206	**write**:
207	```json
208	{
209	  "id": "save_result",
210	  "kind": "write",
211	  "inputs": [
212	    { "name": "path" },
213	    { "name": "value" }
214	  ],
215	  "ui": { "x": 400, "y": 100 }
216	}
217	```
218	
219	## Inspector behavior
220	
221	### `read`
222	
223	- **Inline value** field (textarea / text input): a free-form
224	  literal that, when non-empty, becomes the box's output and
225	  HIDES the `path` input port on the canvas. Same hide-when-
226	  literal precedence is the obvious progression of issue 235's
227	  "literal value replaces port name" idea, applied to the port
228	  itself.
229	- **Path** field: the literal-on-port equivalent for the `path`
230	  input. Disabled when the inline value is set.
231	- The two fields are mutually-illuminating: typing into one
232	  visibly disables the other so the user always knows which
233	  mode the box is in.
234	
235	### `write`
236	
237	- Two input port rows (`path`, `value`) with the standard
238	  port literal / wire UI.
239	- No inline-value field (a `write` with no value to commit
240	  is meaningless).
241	
242	### Kind dropdown
243	
244	Replace the current `['call', 'data']` list with
245	`['call', 'read', 'write']`. Picking a kind from this dropdown
246	swaps the box's input/output shape per the schemas above.
247	
248	## Migration
249	
250	Legacy `data` boxes (the cosmetic-only kind) get rewritten by
251	the loader. Two paths:
252	
253	- Boxes with a `ref` / `fn` (the call-equivalent shape they
254	  carry today) → rewrite `kind` to `call`. Behavior identical
255	  to current.
256	- Boxes without a `ref` / `fn` (rare — would be invalid today)
257	  → rewrite to `read` with no path; the validator then flags
258	  them so the user notices and fills in a path or literal.
259	
260	The `data` kind is retired entirely. The schema rejects it
261	after migration runs once.
262	
263	## Unwired-output discard rule
264	
265	The general rule this issue makes use of, applicable to every
266	box:
267	
268	> **If an output value is produced but no wire carries it
269	> downstream, the value is discarded.**
270	
271	A `write` box's done boolean is the most visible case — a user
272	who doesn't need a chain-after-write trigger leaves the output
273	unwired and the runtime drops the value. The rule already holds
274	informally for every box; documenting it here (and in
275	`docs/001-architecture.md`) makes it explicit.
276	
277	## Suggested implementation sequence
278	
279	1. **Block on 317**: cross-language emit/write callbacks must be
280	   in place. Without them this issue degrades to plain-text IO.
281	2. **Schema** (`src/001-schema.lua`, `src/010-graph-loader.c`):
282	   accept `read` and `write`; retire `data`.
283	3. **Loader migration** (`src/003-loader.lua`): rewrite legacy
284	   `data` boxes per the migration section.
285	4. **Phase 2 executor** (`src/004-executor.lua`): handle `read`
286	   (literal or file) and `write` directly. Cross-language
287	   conversion uses the spec callbacks via the same path as
288	   call-box value transport.
289	5. **Phase 3 dispatch** (`src/012-dispatch.c`): same handling at
290	   the dispatch layer. `read`'s fan-out loops through downstream
291	   wires per spec; `write` looks up source-spec on its `value`
292	   input.
293	6. **Inspector** (`assets/js/004-inspector.js`): kind-specific
294	   layout per "Inspector behavior" above. The inline-value field
295	   for `read` is new; the port-hide-when-literal-value behavior
296	   is new and needs a small render-side hook in `draw_box`.
297	7. **Box rendering** (`assets/js/002-boxes.js`):
298	   - Add `read: '#4caf7d'` and `write: '#d4762a'` to `KIND_COLOR`.
299	   - Add `read`/`write` to the `KIND_HEADER` map from issue 236.
300	   - Honor `hide_port` flag (or equivalent) on the path input
301	     when the inline value is set on a `read` box.
302	8. **Documentation**: update `docs/001-architecture.md` with the
303	   new kinds, the unwired-output rule, and the language-aware
304	   IO behavior. Update the dispatch-layer info.md.
305	
306	## Open questions
307	
308	- **Output port labels** for `read` and `write`: `contents` for
309	  read, `done` for write — pick once, document, move on. Issue
310	  236 already established the per-kind output-label table; add
311	  these two rows.
312	- **Static path on `write`**: should `write` also accept an
313	  inline literal path on its `path` port (same way `read`
314	  does)? Probably yes — the use case "save to a fixed location
315	  every time" is common. Comes free if we apply the existing
316	  port-literal-value mechanism.
317	- **`read` with both literal and path set on disk** (a user who
318	  edits the JSON by hand and sets both): validator rejects, or
319	  literal-wins? Validator rejects — explicit error rather than
320	  hidden precedence.
321	- **Cross-language type fidelity** for the C spec: walking
322	  arbitrary C structs is rabbit-hole territory (issue 317
323	  tracks this). Until C's `native_to_json` handles structs,
324	  C-source `write` boxes are limited to scalar values. Same
325	  limitation issue 317 documents.
326	
327	## Relevant files
328	
329	- `src/001-schema.lua` — kind validation, new shapes
330	- `src/010-graph-loader.c` — C-side schema, migration
331	- `src/003-loader.lua` — legacy `data` rewrite
332	- `src/004-executor.lua` — phase 2 runtime handling
333	- `src/012-dispatch.c` — phase 3 dispatch primitives
334	- `assets/js/004-inspector.js` — kind-specific inspector layout
335	- `assets/js/002-boxes.js` — color, header, port-hide on literal
336	- `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.c` — `native_to_json` / `json_to_native`
337	  (issue 317 owns these)
338	- `docs/001-architecture.md` — kinds section, unwired-output rule
339	- `issues/317-spec-json-bridge-for-data-boxes.md` — hard dependency
340	- `issues/235-...completed/` — literal-replaces-port-name idea
341	  extended to "literal hides port entirely" here
342	- `issues/236-...completed/` — per-kind header/output-label map
343	- `issues/completed/216-read-file-box.md` — Lua-side read superseded
344	- `issues/304-task-dispatch-layer.md` — phase 3 home for these
345	- `issues/completed/109-data-files-persistent-and-ephemeral.md` —
346	  `soramech-data` is a separate concern; not affected
347	
348	---
349	
350	## Historical proposals (superseded)
351	
352	### Revision 2 (single `data` kind, literal-or-file)
353	
354	Proposed one unified source box: literal value typed in the
355	inspector OR file at `path`. Plus a separate `write` sink.
356	Rejected because the source-and-sink-in-one-kind ambiguity
357	analysis (above, in "Why two kinds") flagged real footguns —
358	the `path`-input-without-`value` case silently reading from
359	a file the user meant to write to.
360	
361	### Revision 1 (`data` source + `file_write` sink)
362	
363	Proposed a `data` kind (no inputs, just a `path` field; emits
364	file bytes) and a separate write-sink. Renamed in revision 2
365	to `read` / `write` because verbs match what the boxes do
366	better than `data` does. Both revisions kept the same source
367	/ sink split.
368	
369	The binary-data, atomic-write, JSON-parsing, and dispatch-layer
370	home questions all carry over unchanged through every
371	revision.
372	
```

-- Synchronous FSM executor for the SoraMech runner.
-- Walks the graph from the entry box, invokes language drivers for each
-- box in dependency order, and threads output values through wires.
-- Each box call is isolated in run_task() — the future thread pool swap point.

               DIR .. "/src/?.lua;" ..
               package.path

local json = require("dkjson")

-- {{{ shell_quote
local function shell_quote(s)
    -- wrap a string in single quotes, escaping any embedded single quotes
    return "'" .. tostring(s):gsub("'", "'\\''") .. "'"
end
-- }}}

-- {{{ driver_path_for
local function driver_path_for(ref, drivers)
    -- binary callers (no extension mapping needed — they are executable directly)
    -- source callers: look up extension in driver table
    local ext = ref:match("%.([^./]+)$")
    if not ext then return ref end  -- no extension: treat as binary
    local driver = drivers["." .. ext]
    return driver
end
```

371	/* The push triggers spawn_if_ready on the consumer. Tag = 0 for
372	 * the plain fan-out case; iterator routing uses push_branch with a
373	 * non-zero tag. */
374	static int push_to_downstream(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
375	                              const void *out_bytes, int out_size)
376	{
377	    for (int i = 0; i < b->n_connections; i++) {
378	        if (push_one_connection(ctx, b, &b->connections[i],
379	                                out_bytes, out_size, 0) != 0) return -1;
380	    }
381	    return 0;
382	}

385	/* {{{ parse_double() — parse a NUL-bounded byte run as a double */
386	static double parse_double(const void *bytes, int size)
387	{
388	    char tmp[64];
389	    int n = size < 63 ? size : 63;
390	    if (n > 0) memcpy(tmp, bytes, (size_t)n);
391	    tmp[n] = '\0';
392	    return strtod(tmp, NULL);
393	}
394	/* }}} */
395	
396	/* {{{ push_routed() — branch picker over routing.kind, then push */
397	static int push_routed(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
398	                       const void *out_bytes, int out_size)
399	{
400	    /* Data and file_write boxes don't carry a routing kind; their
401	     * output fans plain. Same for call boxes with no routing set
402	     * (treated as plain). */
403	    if (b->kind != BOX_CALL) return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
404	
405	    switch (b->routing.kind) {
406	        case ROUTING_PLAIN:
407	            return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
408	
409	        case ROUTING_COMPARATOR: {
410	            double v = parse_double(out_bytes, out_size);
411	            const char *branch =
412	                (v < b->routing.comparand) ? "lt" :
413	                (v > b->routing.comparand) ? "gt" : "eq";
414	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, 0) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
415	        }
416	
417	        case ROUTING_ITERATOR: {
418	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1) {
419	                /* No counter slot or zero outputs — fall back to plain. */
420	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
421	            }
422	            /* The counter is both the branch selector AND the order
423	             * tag for the consumer slot (issue 304 cell-tagged
424	             * ordering). Tagged consumers serve pops by ascending
425	             * tag, so parallel iterator tasks land in invocation
426	             * order regardless of which worker finishes first. */
427	            uint32_t idx = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id,
428	                                         (uint32_t)b->routing.n_outputs);
429	            char branch[24];
430	            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, "out_%u", idx);
431	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, idx) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
432	        }
433	
434	        case ROUTING_RANDOMIZER: {
435	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1) {
436	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
437	            }
438	            /* Mix the monotonic counter so consecutive invocations
439	             * don't go to consecutive branches. xorshift32-style,
440	             * no crypto pretensions. */
441	            uint32_t i = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id, UINT32_MAX);
442	            uint32_t h = i;
443	            h ^= h >> 16; h *= 0x85ebca6bu;
444	            h ^= h >> 13; h *= 0xc2b2ae35u;
445	            h ^= h >> 16;
446	            uint32_t branch_idx = h % (uint32_t)b->routing.n_outputs;
447	            char branch[24];
448	            snprintf(branch, sizeof branch, "out_%u", branch_idx);
449	            /* Randomizer is also multi-spawn-aware; pass i as tag so
450	             * any downstream tagged slot still serves in order. */
451	            return push_branch(ctx, b, branch, out_bytes, out_size, i) < 0 ? -1 : 0;
452	        }
453	
454	        case ROUTING_WEIGHTED: {
455	            if (b->counter_slot_id < 0 || b->routing.n_outputs < 1 ||
456	                !b->routing.weights) {
457	                return push_to_downstream(ctx, b, out_bytes, out_size);
458	            }
459	            /* Cumulative-band lookup against a counter scaled to a
460	             * fixed precision. The last band absorbs floating-point
461	             * rounding so the bands sum to the whole range. */
462	            enum { WEIGHTED_PRECISION = 1000 };
463	            uint32_t i = slot_read_inc(ctx->slots, b->counter_slot_id,
464	                                       (uint32_t)WEIGHTED_PRECISION);
465	            double total = 0.0;
466	            for (int k = 0; k < b->routing.n_outputs; k++) {
467	                total += b->routing.weights[k];

323	/* The `tag` is the ordering hint for cell-tagged consumer slots
324	 * (issue 304's iterator-ordering rule). For pushes from
325	 * non-iterator producers it's 0; for iterator pushes the routing
326	 * code passes the counter value so parallel iterator tasks
327	 * preserve invocation order at the consumer. */
328	static int push_one_connection(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
329	                               const connection_t *c,
330	                               const void *out_bytes, int out_size,
331	                               uint32_t tag)
332	{
333	    if (c->to_box_idx < 0) return 0;
334	    const box_t *dst = graph_box(ctx->graph, c->to_box_idx);
335	    if (!dst || !dst->input_slot_ids) return 0;
336	    if (c->to_input_idx < 0 || c->to_input_idx >= dst->n_inputs) return 0;
337	    if (slot_push(ctx->slots, dst->input_slot_ids[c->to_input_idx],
338	                  out_bytes, out_size, tag) != 0) {
339	        fprintf(stderr, "dispatch: '%s' → '%s'.%s: push failed\n",
340	                b->id, dst->id, c->to_input);
341	        return -1;

343	    dispatch_spawn_if_ready(ctx, c->to_box_idx, 0);
344	    return 0;
345	}

349	/* `branch` is one of "lt" / "eq" / "gt" / "out_<n>". Connections
350	 * whose from_branch is NULL (plain) are skipped; connections whose
351	 * from_branch equals `branch` fire. */
352	static int push_branch(dispatch_ctx_t *ctx, const box_t *b,
353	                       const char *branch,
354	                       const void *out_bytes, int out_size,
355	                       uint32_t tag)
356	{
357	    int fired = 0;
358	    for (int i = 0; i < b->n_connections; i++) {
359	        const connection_t *c = &b->connections[i];
360	        if (!c->from_branch) continue;
361	        if (strcmp(c->from_branch, branch) != 0) continue;
362	        if (push_one_connection(ctx, b, c, out_bytes, out_size, tag) != 0)
363	            return -1;
364	        fired++;
365	    }
366	    return fired;
367	}

599	    while (total < out_capacity) {

601	                         (size_t)(out_capacity - total), fp);
602	        if (r == 0) break;
603	        total += (int)r;
604	    }
605	    fclose(fp);
606	    free(full);

608	    return 0;

### [602] type=assistant
ts: 2026-05-20T03:28:05.987Z

Before I start, one finding to share: the deep "language-aware IO" piece (true native value handoff at the wire, per the issue's "Calls that language's `json_to_native` to lift the value into the target language's native form") needs a slot-store refactor — the slot today carries bytes, and the bridges I built use the lua_State stack convention, not byte-in/byte-out. Bridging that gap means either:

- Adding a "native handle" alternative to the slot store (slot carries either bytes or a "look at top of producer's stack" handle), or
- Refactoring the invoke chain so producers leave values on the stack and dispatch consumes them via the bridges

Either is a deeper refactor than 229. So this round delivers everything else in option C — kind rename, inline literal on read, boolean done on write, editor surface, fixtures — and the "language-aware IO" piece becomes a documented next slice in the 229 issue file. The byte-passing model still works end-to-end the way it does today, just with cleaner kind names.

Proceeding with that scope. Starting with `010-graph-loader.h`.

```
1	/* src/012-dispatch.c — task dispatch layer, implementation.
2	 *
3	 * The pool action does five things, in order:
4	 *
5	 *   1. Look up the box from the graph.
6	 *   2. Read each input port's value from its slot (peek mode for
7	 *      this iteration; pop mode + iterator multi-spawn land later).
8	 *   3. Do the box's work:
9	 *        - BOX_CALL — invoke the resolved language spec via the
10	 *          worker's per-language handle.
11	 *        - BOX_DATA — read the file at `box->path` (resolved
12	 *          relative to `graph_map_dir` if non-absolute) and use
13	 *          its contents as the output.
14	 *        - BOX_FILE_WRITE — write the second input ("text") to the
15	 *          path given by the first input or by box->path.
16	 *   4. Push the resulting bytes to every outgoing connection's
17	 *      consumer input slot.
18	 *   5. For each consumer, fire the spawn-on-input-ready check,
19	 *      which spawns the consumer iff it has all required inputs
20	 *      and hasn't been spawned yet (single-spawn guard via an
21	 *      atomic CAS).
22	 *
23	 * The optional `capture_outputs` mode also memcpy's the output
24	 * bytes into `ctx->last_outputs[box_id]` so tests can verify
25	 * what each box produced after the pool quiesces.
26	 *
27	 * Designed in issue 304.
28	 */
29	
30	#include "012-dispatch.h"
```
